# Latin America

"Latinoamérica", "América Latina", and "LatAm" redirect here. For the demonym, see [Latin Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Americans). For the song, see [Latinoamérica (song)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latinoam%C3%A9rica_\(song\)). For the film, see [*America Latina* (film)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_Latina_\(film\)). For the airline, see [LATAM Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LATAM_Airlines). For other uses, see [LATAM (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LATAM_\(disambiguation\)).

**Latin America** ([Spanish](26825.md): *América Latina* or *Latinoamérica*; [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language): *América Latina*) is the cultural region of the Americas where [Romance languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_languages) are predominantly spoken, primarily Spanish and Portuguese.[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10]

Since it is defined according to its cultural identity instead of its geography, Latin America includes countries from both [North](21139.md) and [South America](26769.md). For instance, countries from North America like [Mexico](3966054.md), [Honduras](13394.md), [Guatemala](17238567.md), etc. tend to be included. In fact, most of the countries in Central America, South America and the [Caribbean](18956035.md) are considered Latin American countries. English or [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language)-speaking countries in South America or the Caribbean like [Belize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belize), [Guyana](17238691.md), [Jamaica](15660.md), [Trinidad and Tobago](3565457.md), [Suriname](26828.md), etc. are not considered "Latin American" because they speak Germanic languages. As such, Latin America comprises [Hispanic America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_America) along with [Brazil](3383.md).[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13] Related terms include the narrower Hispanic America, which exclusively refers to Spanish-speaking nations; and the broader [Ibero-America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibero-America), which includes all Iberian countries in the Americas.[^cite_note-14]

The term *Latin America* was first introduced in 1856 at a conference named *Initiative of the Americas: Idea for a Federal Congress of the Republics* (*Iniciativa de la América. Idea de un Congreso Federal de las Repúblicas*), at Paris.[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-bilbao_f-16] Chilean politician [Francisco Bilbao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Bilbao) coined the term to refer to countries sharing cultural and linguistic heritage. It gained further prominence during the 1860s under the rule of [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III), whose government sought to justify France's intervention in the [Second Mexican Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Mexican_Empire).[^cite_note-Britton-17]

## Etymology and definitions



The first page of [Nebrija's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_de_Nebrija) *[Gramática de la lengua castellana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gram%C3%A1tica_de_la_lengua_castellana)* in 1492.

Latin America is a geographical region where Spanish or Portuguese is the national language with a common culture, and tradition. As a whole, it can be traced back to the 1830s, in the writing of the French [Saint-Simonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Simonianism) [Michel Chevalier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Chevalier), who postulated that a part of the Americas was inhabited by people of a "[Latin race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_peoples)", and that it could, therefore, ally itself with "[Latin Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Europe)", ultimately overlapping the [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church), in a struggle with "[Teutonic Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic-speaking_Europe)" and "[Anglo-Saxon America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-America)" with its [Anglo-Saxonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxonism), as well as "[Slavic Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_Europe)" with its [Pan-Slavism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Slavism).[^cite_note-mignolo-18]

Published in 149&#x32;*, [Gramática de la lengua castellana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gram%C3%A1tica_de_la_lengua_castellana)* (lit. 'Grammar of the [Castilian Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castilian_languages)') written by [Antonio de Nebrija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_de_Nebrija) was the first work dedicated to the Spanish language and its rules, and it was the first [grammar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammar) of a modern [European language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Europe) to be published.[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20]

[Uruguayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruguayan) historian [Arturo Ardao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Ardao) and [Chilean](9542325.md) historian [Miguel Rojas Mix](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel%20Rojas%20Mix) found evidence that the term "Latin America" was used earlier than [John Leddy Phelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Leddy_Phelan) had claimed, and that the first use of the term was in opposition to imperialist projects in the Americas. Ardao wrote about this subject in his book *Génesis de la idea y el nombre de América latina* (Genesis of the Idea and the Name of Latin America, 1980),[^cite_note-21] and Miguel Rojas Mix in his article "*Bilbao y el hallazgo de América latina: Unión continental, socialista y libertaria*" (Bilbao and the Finding of Latin America: a Continental, Socialist, and Libertarian Union, 1986).[^cite_note-22]

Michel Gobat notes that "Arturo Ardao, Miguel Rojas Mix, and Aims McGuinness have revealed \[that] the term 'Latin America' had already been used in 1856 by Central Americans and South Americans protesting US expansion into the [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere)".[^cite_note-23] Edward Shawcross summarizes Ardao's and Rojas Mix's findings: "Ardao identified the term in a poem by a Colombian diplomat and intellectual resident in France, José María Torres Caicedo, published on 15 February 1857 in a French-based Spanish-language newspaper, while Rojas Mix located it in a speech delivered in France by the radical liberal Chilean politician [Francisco Bilbao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Bilbao) in June 1856".[^cite_note-24]

By the late 1850s, the term was being used in California (by then a part of the United States), with local newspapers such as *El Clamor Público* writing about *América latina* and *latinoamérica*, and identifying as *Latinos* as the abbreviated term for their "hemispheric membership in *la raza latina*".[^cite_note-Gutierrez-2016-25]

The words "Latin" and "America" were first found to be combined in a printed work to produce the term "Latin America" in 1856 at a conference by the Chilean politician [Francisco Bilbao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Bilbao) in Paris.[^cite_note-26] The conference had the title "Initiative of the America. The idea for a Federal Congress of Republics."[^cite_note-bilbao_f-16] The following year, Colombian writer [José María Torres Caicedo](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9%20Mar%C3%ADa%20Torres%20Caicedo) also used the term in his poem "The Two Americas".[^cite_note-27]

Both authors advocated for the political and economic union of all Latin American countries, arguing that regional unity was the only effective means of defending their territories against further foreign interventions by the United States. They also rejected European imperialism, warning that the resurgence of non-democratic governments in Europe posed an additional threat to the stability and autonomy of Latin American nations. In criticizing European political developments, they employed the same term to characterize the deteriorating state of European governance at the time: "despotism".l c[^cite_note-28]

Several years later, during the [French invasion of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_French_intervention_in_Mexico), Bilbao wrote another work, "Emancipation of the Spirit in the Americas", where he asked all Latin American countries to support the Mexican cause against France, and rejected French imperialism in Asia, Africa, Europe and the Americas. He asked Latin American intellectuals to search for their "intellectual emancipation" by abandoning all French ideas, claiming that France was: "Hypocrite, because she \[France] calls herself protector of the Latin race just to subject it to her exploitation regime; treacherous, because she speaks of freedom and nationality, when, unable to conquer freedom for herself, she enslaves others instead!" Therefore, as Michel Gobat puts it, the term Latin America itself had an "anti-imperial genesis," and their creators were far from supporting any form of imperialism in the region, or in any other place of the globe.[^cite_note-29]

Following in the tradition of Chilean writer Francisco Bilbao, who excluded Brazil, Argentina and Paraguay from his early conceptualization of Latin America,[^cite_note-30] Chilean historian [Jaime Eyzaguirre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaime_Eyzaguirre) has criticized the term Latin America for "disguising" and "diluting" the Spanish character of a region (i.e. [Hispanic America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_America)) with the inclusion of nations that, according to him, do not share the same pattern of [conquest and colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas).[^cite_note-31] Historian Mauricio Tenorio-Trillo remarked, "the idea of 'Latin America' ought to have vanished with the obsolescence of racial theory."[^cite_note-32]

### Modern definitions



Areas of the world with a [Catholic Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Christian) majority. Latin America, like [Latin Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Europe), is linked to the [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church).

- The most common definition of Latin America is the countries and territories in the Americas where the populations speak Spanish or Portuguese and the dominant religion is [Roman Catholic](606848.md), with it being synonymous with Ibero-America ("Iberian America"). Puerto Rico, the Spanish-speaking Caribbean territory of the United States, acquired from the [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) following its defeat in the 1898 [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War), is usually included. This definition excludes the predominantly Protestant English-speaking and Dutch-speaking regions, as well as French-speaking predominantly Catholic regions. Belize, Guyana and Suriname, as well as several French overseas departments, are excluded from the definition.

  [^cite_note-33]

- In a less used definition, Latin America designates the set of countries in the Americas where any Romance language (a language derived from Latin) predominates: Spanish, Portuguese, or French. Thus, it includes Mexico; most of Central and South America; and in the Caribbean, Cuba, Puerto Rico, the Dominican Republic, and Haiti. [French Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Guiana) and the [French West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_West_Indies) are sometimes included. By this definition, *Latin America* then comprises all of the countries in the Americas that were once part of the [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire), [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire), and [French Empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_French_Empire).

  [^cite_note-34]

  [^cite_note-Torres-35]

- The term is sometimes used more broadly to refer to all of the Americas south of the United States, thus including [the Guianas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guianas) ([French Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Guiana), Guyana, and Suriname); the [Anglophone Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Caribbean) (and Belize); the [Francophone Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_West_Indies); and the [Dutch Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Caribbean). This definition emphasizes a similar [socioeconomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socioeconomics) history of the region, which was characterized by formal or [informal colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neocolonialism), rather than cultural aspects (see, for example, [dependency theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dependency_theory)).

  [^cite_note-36]

  [^cite_note-37]

  [^cite_note-38]

  [^cite_note-39]

  [^cite_note-40]

  [^cite_note-41]

  [^cite_note-42]

  &#x20;Some sources use the alternative phrase "[Latin America and the Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_America_and_the_Caribbean)", as in the [United Nations geoscheme for the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_geoscheme_for_the_Americas).

  [^cite_note-43]

[Quebec](7954867.md) and [Acadia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acadia), [Francophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographical_distribution_of_French_speakers) parts of North America, are generally excluded from the definition of Latin America.[^cite_note-46]

### Subregions and countries



The four common subregions in Latin America

Latin America can be subdivided into several subregions based on geography, politics, [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_the_Americas), demographics and culture. The basic geographical subregions are North America, Central America, the [Caribbean](18956035.md) and South America; the latter contains further politico-geographical subdivisions such as the [Southern Cone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cone), [the Guianas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guianas) and the [Andean civilizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andean_civilizations). It may be subdivided on linguistic grounds into [Spanish America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_America) and [Portuguese America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_America).[^cite_note-GeoSEP-47]

## History

Main articles: [History of Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Latin_America) and [Pre-Columbian era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Columbian_era)



[Mayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_Civilization) UNESCO [World Heritage Site](44940.md) of [Chichén Itzá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chich%C3%A9n_Itz%C3%A1).



[Philip II's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain) dominions in 1598, coloured by governing council:   [Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Castile)   [Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Aragon)   [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Portugal)   [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Italy)   [the Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Indies)   [Flanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Flanders)&#x20;

Before the [arrival of Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas) in the late 15th and early 16th centuries, the region was home to many indigenous peoples, including advanced civilizations, most notably from South: the [Olmec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olmec), [Maya](18449273.md), [Muisca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muisca_people), [Aztecs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztecs) and [Inca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inca_civilization). The region came under control of the kingdoms of Spain and Portugal which established colonies, and imposed [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism) and their languages. These countries brought African [slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave) to their colonies as laborers, exploiting large, settled societies and their resources. The Spanish Crown regulated immigration, allowing only Christians to travel to the New World.[^cite_note-50]

The colonization process led to significant native population declines due to disease, forced labor, and violence. They imposed their culture, destroying native codices and artwork. Colonial-era religion played a crucial role in everyday life, with the Spanish Crown ensuring religious purity and aggressively prosecuting perceived deviations like [witchcraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witchcraft_in_Latin_America). The [decline of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_Spain) was the gradual process of financial and military exhaustion and attrition suffered by [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) throughout the 17th century, in particular when viewed in comparison with ascendant rival powers of the [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France) and [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England).[^cite_note-devries-51]

Because Catholicism had played such an important role in the *[Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista)* (Catholic reconquest) of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) from the Muslims, the Catholic Church in essence became another arm of the Spanish government, since the crown was granted [sweeping powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronato_real) over ecclesiastical affairs in its overseas territories. The Spanish Crown granted it a large role in the administration of the state, and this practice became even more pronounced in the New World, where prelates often assumed the role of government officials.[^cite_note-Oto-Peralías2016-52]

Under [Philip II of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain), royal power over Latin America increased, but the crown knew little about its overseas possessions. Although the [Council of the Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Indies) was tasked with oversight there, it acted without advice of high officials with direct colonial experience. Another serious problem was that the crown did not know what Spanish laws were in force there.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParker2014-53]

### Spanish Golden Age

Main article: [Spanish Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Golden_Age)



[The library of El Escorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_the_Monastery_of_San_Lorenzo_de_El_Escorial)

The [Spanish Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Golden_Age) was a period of intense artistic, literary, and intellectual flourishing in Spain is generally associated with the reigns of [Isabella I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabella_I_of_Castile), [Ferdinand II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon), [Charles V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor), [Philip II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain), [Philip III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_III_of_Spain), and [Philip IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_IV_of_Spain), during which Spain became a leading political and military power.[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] The Spanish Golden Age is conventionally considered to have begun in 1492, and is often regarded as having ended in 1659 with the [Treaty of the Pyrenees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_the_Pyrenees), which marked the decline of Spanish hegemony in Europe, although the period is commonly understood to have continued in cultural terms until 1681, the year of [Pedro Calderón de la Barca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Calder%C3%B3n_de_la_Barca)'s death.[^cite_note-57]

The era was characterized by extensive royal, aristocratic, and ecclesiastical patronage, which supported literature, painting, architecture, music, and theatre.[^cite_note-Salamanca-58] Major artistic and cultural projects, including [El Escorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Escorial), contributed to the formation of a distinctive Spanish court and religious culture. The period encompassed the [Plateresque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plateresque) and [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) styles and extended into the early [Spanish Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Baroque_painting).[^cite_note-59] It produced many of the best-known figures in Spanish cultural history, including [Miguel de Cervantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_de_Cervantes), [Lope de Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lope_de_Vega), [Luis de Góngora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_de_G%C3%B3ngora), [Francisco de Quevedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_de_Quevedo), [Diego Velázquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez), and composers such as [Tomás Luis de Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Luis_de_Victoria) and [Francisco Guerrero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Guerrero_\(composer\)).[^cite_note-60]

### Independence movements

In the early nineteenth century nearly all of areas of [Spanish America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_America) attained independence by armed struggle, with the exceptions of Cuba and Puerto Rico. Brazil, which had become a monarchy separate from Portugal, became a republic in the late nineteenth century. Political independence from European monarchies did not result in the abolition of black slavery in the new nations, and it resulted in political and economic instability in Spanish America immediately after independence. Yet, as regional [Caudillos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caudillo) started to rise in power, nation-builders started to view themselves as more modern than their former European colonizers. Leaders began to shift away from aristocracy toward republicanism and democracy, which allowed all citizens, not just the Creole elites, to have a voice in politics. This shift helped unify many of the Latin American nations as all people, even illiterate people, would gather in their communities to talk about political ideals and how they should be used in their nation.[^cite_note-61] Great Britain and the United States exercised significant influence in the post-independence era, resulting in a form of [neo-colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-colonialism), where political sovereignty remained in place, but foreign powers exercised considerable power in the economic sphere. Newly independent nations faced domestic and interstate conflicts, struggling with economic instability and social inequality.

The 20th century brought U.S. intervention and the [Cold War](325329.md)'s impact on the region, with revolutions in countries like Cuba influencing Latin American politics. The late 20th and early 21st centuries saw shifts towards left-wing governments, followed by conservative resurgences, and a recent resurgence of left-wing politics in several countries.

### After 2000



[UNASUR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNASUR) summit in the Palacio de la Moneda, [Santiago de Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_de_Chile)

In many countries in the early 2000s, left-wing political parties rose to power, known as the [Pink tide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pink_tide). The presidencies of [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) (1999–2013) in Venezuela, [Ricardo Lagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Lagos) and [Michelle Bachelet](986814.md) in Chile, [Lula da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lula_da_Silva) and [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff) of the [Workers Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers_Party_\(Brazil\)) (PT) in Brazil, [Néstor Kirchner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%A9stor_Kirchner) and his wife [Cristina Fernández](1644780.md) in Argentina, [Tabaré Vázquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabar%C3%A9_V%C3%A1zquez) and [José Mujica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mujica) in Uruguay, [Evo Morales](284163.md) in Bolivia, [Daniel Ortega](8778.md) in Nicaragua, [Rafael Correa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Correa) in Ecuador, [Fernando Lugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Lugo) in Paraguay, [Manuel Zelaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Zelaya) in Honduras (removed from power by a [coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Honduran_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)), [Mauricio Funes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauricio_Funes) and [Salvador Sánchez Cerén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_S%C3%A1nchez_Cer%C3%A9n) in El Salvador are all part of this wave of left-wing politicians who often declare themselves [socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism), [Latin Americanists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_integration), or [anti-imperialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Imperialism), often implying [opposition to US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Americanism#Latin_America) [policies towards the region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_consensus). An aspect of this has been the creation of the eight-member ALBA alliance, or "[The Bolivarian Alliance for the Peoples of Our America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_Alliance_for_the_Americas)" (Spanish: *Alianza Bolivariana para los Pueblos de Nuestra América*) by some of these countries.[^cite_note-62]



Honduran demonstrator holding a banner with a "don't turn left" sign, 2009

Following the pink tide, there was a [Conservative wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_wave) across Latin America. In Mexico, the rightwing [National Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Action_Party_\(Mexico\)) (PAN) won the [presidential election of 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Mexican_general_election) with its candidate [Vicente Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Fox), ending the 71-year rule of the [Institutional Revolutionary Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_Revolutionary_Party). He was succeeded six years later by another conservative, [Felipe Calderón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Calder%C3%B3n) (2006–2012), who attempted to crack down on the Mexican drug cartels and instigated the [Mexican drug war](8442323.md). Several right-wing leaders rose to power, including Argentina's [Mauricio Macri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauricio_Macri) and Brazil's [Michel Temer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Temer), following the impeachment of the country's first female president. In Chile, the conservative [Sebastián Piñera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebasti%C3%A1n_Pi%C3%B1era) succeeded the socialist [Michelle Bachelet](986814.md) in 2017.[^cite_note-63] In 2019, center-right [Luis Lacalle Pou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Lacalle_Pou) ended a 15-year leftist rule in Uruguay, after defeating the [Broad Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broad_Front_\(Uruguay\)) candidate.[^cite_note-64]

Economically, the [2000s commodities boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_commodities_boom) caused positive effects for many Latin American economies. Another trend was the rapidly increasing importance of their [relations with China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Latin_America_relations).[^cite_note-AFP2011-65] However, with the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) beginning in 2008, there was an end to the commodity boom, resulting in [economic stagnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_stagnation) or [recession](25382.md) in some countries. A number of left-wing governments of the Pink tide lost support. The worst-hit was Venezuela, which is facing severe [social and economic upheaval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crisis_in_Bolivarian_Venezuela).[^cite_note-66]

Charges against a major Brazilian conglomerate, [Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odebrecht#Legal_problems), have raised allegations of corruption across the region's governments (see [Operation Car Wash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Car_Wash)). This bribery ring has become the largest corruption scandal in Latin American history.[^cite_note-67] As of July 2017, the highest ranking politicians charged were former Brazilian President [Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](99328.md), who was arrested,[^cite_note-68] and former Peruvian presidents [Ollanta Humala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ollanta_Humala) and [Alejandro Toledo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Toledo), who fled to the United States and was extradited back to Peru.[^cite_note-69]

The [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) proved a political challenge for many unstable Latin American democracies, with scholars identifying a decline in civil liberties as a result of opportunistic emergency powers. This was especially true for countries with strong presidential regimes, such as Brazil.[^cite_note-70]

## Geography

### Neotropical realm

Main article: [Neotropical realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neotropical_realm)



The [Neotropical realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neotropical_realm) (in purple)

Most of Latin America is part of the [Neotropical realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neotropical_realm), one of the eight [biogeographic realms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogeographic_realm) of Earth's land surface. The Neotropic is delimited by similarities in [fauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna) or [flora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora), which are distinct from the [Nearctic realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nearctic_realm), which includes most of North America. The Neotropic includes more [tropical rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest) ([tropical and subtropical moist broadleaf forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_moist_broadleaf_forests)) than any other realm. It extends from southern Mexico through Central America and northern South America to [southern Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Brazil), including the vast [Amazon rainforest](48139.md). These rainforest ecoregions are one of the most important reserves of [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity) on Earth.[^cite_note-71]

### Climate

Main article: [Geography of South America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_South_America)



South America Köppen climate map

[^cite_note-72]

As part of the [Hadley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadley_cell) model of atmospheric circulation, the equator is characterised by the ascending branches of separate [meridional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zonal_and_meridional_flow) cells, driven by intense [insolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_irradiance).[^cite_note-Sylvestre2009-73][^cite_note-Grodsky2003-74] The vertical [convection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convection) draws in air from the surrounding atmosphere, known as the [trade winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_winds). As these inward flows of air converge they reduce horizontal wind speed, and as they rise, they form [precipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation). The vertical convection also results in a net export of heat and freshwater from the lower atmosphere into the [troposphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troposphere).[^cite_note-Sylvestre2009-73][^cite_note-Oliver2005-75]

The [Great American Interchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_American_Interchange) was an important late [Cenozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cenozoic) paleozoogeographic [biotic interchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biotic_interchange) event in which land and freshwater fauna migrated from North America to South America via Central America and vice versa, as the volcanic [Isthmus of Panama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isthmus_of_Panama) rose up from the sea floor, forming a [land bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_bridge) between the previously separated continents. Although earlier dispersals had occurred, probably over water, the migration accelerated dramatically about 2.7 million years ([Ma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_\(unit\))) ago during the [Piacenzian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piacenzian) age. It resulted from the joining of the Neotropic (roughly South American) and Nearctic (roughly North American) biogeographic realms to definitively form the Americas.[^cite_note-ODea2016-76]

### Environment

See also: [Environmental history of Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_history_of_Latin_America)

The environment of Latin America has been changed by human use in the expanding of agriculture, new agricultural technologies, including the [Green Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution), extraction of minerals, growth of cities, and redirection of rivers by the construction of dams for irrigation, drinking water, and hydroelectric power. In the twenty-first century, there is a growing movement to protect nature, and many governments have sought recognition of natural sites by the [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites). Brazil, Mexico, and Peru currently have the greatest number of natural sites.[^cite_note-list-77]

[Macaws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macaw) are long-tailed, often colorful [New World parrots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neotropical_parrot).

## Economy

Main article: [Latin American economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_economy)

### Economic history

Main article: [Economic history of Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Latin_America)



The former *[Casa Lonja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consulado_de_mercaderes)* (right), built by [Philip II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain) and today the [General Archive of the Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Archive_of_the_Indies), next to the [Cathedral of Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville_Cathedral)



[Potosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerro_Rico) produced massive amounts of silver from a single site in upper Peru. The first image published in Europe. [Pedro Cieza de León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Cieza_de_Le%C3%B3n), 1553.

Before independence from Spain, [international trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade) was regulated by the royal *[Casa de Contratación](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_de_Contrataci%C3%B3n)* (House of Trade) based in [Seville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seville). Inter-regional trade was severely limited with merchants based in Spain and with overseas connections in the main colonial centers controlling the transatlantic trade.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82] Economic thought between 1500 and 1700 was predominantly [mercantilist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilist), such as the [School of Salamanca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Salamanca). This reflected [early modern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_Europe) before the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) but after the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance).[^cite_note-Salamanca-58]

British traders began making inroads into the theoretically closed Spanish system in the eighteenth century,[^cite_note-83] and the Spanish crown instituted a series of changes in policy in the eighteenth century, known as the [Bourbon Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Reforms), designed to bring the Spanish America under closer crown control. However, one innovation was *comercio libre* ("free commerce"), which was not [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade) as generally understood, but allowed all Spanish and Spanish American ports to be accessible to each other, excluding foreign traders, in a move to stimulate economic activity yet maintain crown control.[^cite_note-84]

At independence in the early nineteenth century, Spanish America and Brazil had no foreign investment or direct, legal contact with economic partners beyond those allowed within controlled trade.[^cite_note-85] Unlike the later [East India Companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company), which were chartered companies established by the [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Empire), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire), and others, the *Casa* collected all colonial taxes and duties, approved all voyages of exploration and trade, maintained secret information on trade routes and new discoveries, licensed captains, and administered [commercial law](77444.md).[^cite_note-FlintFlint2003-86][^cite_note-HendersonDelpar2000-87]

In the period before the 18th century, Latin America's economy was larger than that of [British America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_America), and had a similar level of development. The English and [French colonies in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France) had a temperate climate and no major indigenous populations whose labor could be exploited. France was developing [Colbertism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colbertism) in the 17th century, during the reign of [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV), which would later evolve into *[Dirigisme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirigisme)*.[^cite_note-88]

The Southern United States used plantation agriculture and had large black slave populations, sharing more characteristics with Brazil and the Caribbean than the northern English colonies. The main difference with Latin America is that the southern English colonies are predominantly Protestant to this day, forming a "[Bible Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Belt)," rather than Catholic.[^cite_note-89] The northern English colonies were characterized by the family farm, with a homogeneous European-descent population and a much less sharp divide between rich and poor. Unlike Spanish America and Brazil which restricted immigration, the northern English colonies were a magnet for migration, encouraged by the British crown.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid2017-90]
