### Agriculture

Main article: [Agriculture in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Latin_America)



[Soybean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean) plantation in [Mato Grosso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mato_Grosso). In 2020, Brazil was the world's largest producer, with 130 million tons. Latin America produces half of the world's soybeans.

The four countries with the strongest agricultural sector in South America are Brazil, Argentina, Chile and Colombia. Currently:[^cite_note-92]

- Brazil is the world's largest producer of sugarcane, soy, coffee, oranges, [guaraná](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaran%C3%A1), [açaí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C3%A7a%C3%AD) and [Brazil nut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil_nut); is one of the top five producers of maize, [papaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaya), tobacco, [pineapple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineapple), banana, cotton, [beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beans), [coconut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut), [watermelon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watermelon), lemon and [yerba mate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yerba_mate); is one of the top ten world producers of [cocoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa_bean), [cashew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cashew), [avocado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avocado), [tangerine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangerine), [persimmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persimmon), [mango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mango), [guava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guava), rice, [oat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oat), [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum) and tomato; and is one of the top 15 world producers of grapes, apples, melons, peanuts, [figs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_fig), [peaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peach), onions, [palm oil](57561.md) and [natural rubber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_rubber).

- Argentina is the world's largest producer of [yerba mate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yerba_mate); is one of the five largest producers in the world of soy, maize, [sunflower seeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunflower_seed), lemons and pears, one of the 10 largest producers in the world of [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley), grapes, [artichokes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artichoke), tobacco and cotton, and one of the 15 largest producers in the world of wheat, [oats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oat), [chickpeas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickpea), sugarcane, [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum) and [grapefruit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grapefruit).

- Chile is one of the five largest world producers of [cherries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherry) and [cranberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cranberry), and one of the ten largest world producers of grapes, apples, [kiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiwifruit), peaches, [plums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plum) and [hazelnuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazelnut), focusing on exporting high-value fruits.

- Colombia is one of the five largest producers in the world of coffee, [avocados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avocado) and palm oil, and one of the ten largest producers in the world of sugarcane, bananas, [pineapples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineapple) and [cocoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa_bean). It is the main producer of [Coca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca), an illegal plant that is the main ingredient in [Cocaine](7701.md)

- Peru is the world's largest producer of [quinoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinoa); is one of the five largest producers of avocados, [blueberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blueberries), [artichokes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artichoke) and [asparagus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asparagus); one of the ten largest producers in the world of coffee and [cocoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa_bean); one of the 15 largest producers in the world of potatoes and pineapples, and also has a large production of grapes, sugarcane, rice, bananas, maize and [cassava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassava); its agriculture is considerably diversified.

- Paraguay is currently the 6th largest producer of soy in the world and entering the list of the 20 largest producers of maize and sugarcane.

  [^cite_note-93]

In Central America, the following stand out:

- Guatemala is one of the ten largest producers in the world of coffee, sugar cane, melons and [natural rubber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_rubber), and one of the world's 15 largest producers of bananas and [palm oil](57561.md).

- Honduras is one of the five largest producers of coffee in the world, and one of the ten largest producers of [palm oil](57561.md).

- Costa Rica is the world's largest producer of pineapples.

- Dominican Republic is one of the world's top five producers of [papayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaya) and avocados, and one of the ten largest producers of [cocoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa_bean).

- Mexico is the world's largest producer of avocados, one of the world's top five producers of [Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_pepper), lemons, oranges, mangos, papayas, [strawberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strawberry), grapefruit, pumpkins and asparagus, and one of the world's 10 largest producers of sugar cane, maize, [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum), [beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bean), tomatoes, [coconuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut), pineapple, melons and blueberries.



Truck of a meat company in Brazil. Latin America produces 25% of the world's beef and chicken meat.

Brazil is the world's largest exporter of [chicken meat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_meat): 3.77 million tons in 2019.[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95] The country had the second largest herd of cattle in the world, 22.2% of the world herd. The country was the second largest producer of beef in 2019, responsible for 15.4% of global production.[^cite_note-96] It was also the third largest world producer of milk in 2018. This year, the country produced 35.1 billion liters.[^cite_note-97] In 2019, Brazil was the fourth largest pork producer in the world, with almost four million tons.[^cite_note-98]

In 2018, Argentina was the fourth largest producer of beef in the world, with a production of 3 million tons (behind only United States, Brazil and China). Uruguay is also a major meat producer. In 2018, it produced 589 thousand tons of beef.[^cite_note-99]

In the production of [chicken meat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_meat), Mexico is among the ten largest producers in the world, Argentina among the 15 largest and Peru and Colombia among the 20 largest. In beef production, Mexico is one of the ten largest producers in the world and Colombia is one of the 20 largest producers. In the production of pork, Mexico is among the 15 largest producers in the world. In the production of [honey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honey), Argentina is among the five largest producers in the world, Mexico among the ten largest and Brazil among the 15 largest. In terms of [cow's milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cow's_milk) production, Mexico is among the 15 largest producers in the world and Argentina among the 20 largest.[^cite_note-100]

### Mining and petroleum



[Cerro Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerro_Rico), [Potosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potos%C3%AD_Department), Bolivia, still a major mine

&#x20;Mining is one of the most important economic sectors in Latin America, especially for [Chile](22025047.md), [Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Peru) and [Bolivia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Bolivia), whose economies are highly dependent on this sector. The continent has large productions of:

- Gold (mainly in [Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_mining_in_Peru), Mexico, [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_mining_in_Brazil) and Argentina);

  [^cite_note-101]

- Silver (mainly in Mexico, Peru, Chile, Bolivia and Argentina);

  [^cite_note-102]

- Copper (mainly in [Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper_mining_in_Chile), [Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper_mining_in_Peru), Mexico and Brazil);

  [^cite_note-103]

- Iron ore (Brazil, Peru and [Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_mining_in_Chile));

  [^cite_note-104]

- [Zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc) (Peru, Mexico, Bolivia and Brazil);

  [^cite_note-USGS_Zinc_Production_Statistics-105]

- [Molybdenum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molybdenum) (Chile, Peru and Mexico);

  [^cite_note-pubs.usgs.gov-106]

- [Lithium](17561.md) ([Chile](69112591.md), Argentina and Brazil);

  [^cite_note-107]

- Lead (Peru, Mexico and Bolivia);

  [^cite_note-USGS_Lead_Production_Statistics-108]

- [Bauxite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauxite) (Brazil and Jamaica);

  [^cite_note-109]

- [Tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin) (Peru, Bolivia and Brazil);

  [^cite_note-110]

- [Manganese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manganese) (Brazil and Mexico);

  [^cite_note-111]

- [Antimony](898.md) (Bolivia, Mexico, Guatemala and Ecuador);

  [^cite_note-112]

- [Nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel) (Brazil, Dominican Republic and Cuba);

  [^cite_note-113]

- [Niobium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niobium) (Brazil);

  [^cite_note-114]

- [Rhenium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhenium) (Chile);

  [^cite_note-115]

- [Iodine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iodine) ([Chile](22025047.md#Iodine_and_nitrate)),

  [^cite_note-116]

Brazil stands out in the extraction of

- Iron ore (where it is the 2nd largest producer and exporter in the world—iron ore is usually one of the three export products that generate the greatest value in the country's [trade balance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_balance))

- Copper

- Gold

- [Bauxite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauxite) (one of the five largest producers in the world)

- [Manganese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manganese) (one of the five largest producers in the world)

- [Tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin) (one of the largest producers in the world)

- [Niobium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niobium) (98% of known world reserves) and

- [Nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel)

In terms of gemstones, Brazil is the world's largest producer of [amethysts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amethyst), [topaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topaz), and [agates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agate) and one of the main producers of [tourmaline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourmaline), emeralds, [aquamarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquamarine_\(gemstone\)), [garnets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garnet) and [opals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opal).[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122]

Chile contributes about a third of the world's copper production.[^cite_note-123] In addition, Chile was, in 2019, the world's largest producer of iodine[^cite_note-124] and [rhenium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhenium),[^cite_note-125] the second largest producer of [lithium](17561.md)[^cite_note-USGS_Lithium_Production_Statistics-126] and [molybdenum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molybdenum),[^cite_note-pubs.usgs.gov-106] the sixth largest producer of silver,[^cite_note-USGS_Silver_Production_Statistics-127] the seventh largest producer of salt,[^cite_note-USGS_Salt_Production_Statistics-128] the eighth largest producer of [potash](56509.md),[^cite_note-129] the thirteenth-largest producer of [sulfur](27127.md)[^cite_note-130] and the thirteenth largest producer of iron ore[^cite_note-131] in the world.

In 2019, Peru was the second largest world producer of copper[^cite_note-USGS_Copper_Production_Statistics-132] and silver,[^cite_note-USGS_Silver_Production_Statistics-127] 8th largest world producer of gold,[^cite_note-USGS_Gold_Production_Statistics-133] third largest world producer of lead,[^cite_note-USGS_Lead_Production_Statistics-108] second largest world producer of [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc),[^cite_note-USGS_Zinc_Production_Statistics-105] fourth largest world producer of [tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin),[^cite_note-USGS_Tin_Production_Statistics-134] fifth largest world producer of [boron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron),[^cite_note-USGS_Boron_Production_Statistics-135] and fourth largest world producer of molybdenum.[^cite_note-pubs.usgs.gov-106]

In 2019, Bolivia was the eighth largest world producer of silver;[^cite_note-USGS_Silver_Production_Statistics-127] fourth largest world producer of boron;[^cite_note-USGS_Boron_Production_Statistics-135] fifth largest world producer of [antimony](898.md);[^cite_note-136] fifth largest world producer of [tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin);[^cite_note-USGS_Tin_Production_Statistics-134] sixth largest world producer of [tungsten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tungsten);[^cite_note-137] seventh largest producer of [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc),[^cite_note-USGS_Zinc_Production_Statistics-105] and the eighth largest producer of lead.[^cite_note-USGS_Lead_Production_Statistics-108][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139]

In 2019, Mexico was the world's largest producer of silver[^cite_note-USGS_Silver_Production_Statistics-127] (representing almost 23% of world production, producing more than 200 million ounces in 2019);[^cite_note-140] ninth largest producer of gold,[^cite_note-USGS_Gold_Production_Statistics-133] the eighth largest producer of copper,[^cite_note-USGS_Copper_Production_Statistics-132] the world's fifth largest producer of lead,[^cite_note-USGS_Lead_Production_Statistics-108] the world's sixth largest producer of zinc,[^cite_note-USGS_Zinc_Production_Statistics-105] the world's fifth largest producer of molybdenum,[^cite_note-pubs.usgs.gov-106] the world's third largest producer of [mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_\(chemical_element\)),[^cite_note-141] the world's fifth largest producer of [bismuth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bismuth),[^cite_note-142] the world's 13th largest producer of [manganese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manganese)[^cite_note-143] and the 23rd largest world producer of [phosphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphate).[^cite_note-144] It is also the eighth largest world producer of [salt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_salt).[^cite_note-USGS_Salt_Production_Statistics-128]

In 2019, Argentina was the fourth largest world producer of lithium,[^cite_note-USGS_Lithium_Production_Statistics-126] the ninth largest world producer of silver,[^cite_note-USGS_Silver_Production_Statistics-127] the 17th largest world producer of gold[^cite_note-USGS_Gold_Production_Statistics-133] and the seventh largest world producer of boron.[^cite_note-USGS_Boron_Production_Statistics-135]

Colombia is the world's largest producer of [emeralds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emeralds).[^cite_note-145] In the production of gold, between 2006 and 2017, the country produced 15 tons per year until 2007, when its production increased significantly, breaking a record of 66.1 tons extracted in 2012. In 2017, it extracted 52.2 tons. The country is among the 25 largest gold producers in the world.[^cite_note-146] In the production of silver, in 2017 the country extracted 15,5 tons.[^cite_note-147]

In [oil](23195.md) production, Brazil was the tenth largest oil producer in the world in 2019, with 2.8 million barrels a day. Mexico was the twelfth largest, with 2.1 million barrels a day, Colombia in 20th place with 886 thousand barrels a day, Venezuela was the twenty-first place, with 877 thousand barrels a day, Ecuador in 28th with 531 thousand barrels a day and Argentina. 29th with 507 thousand barrels a day. Since Venezuela and Ecuador consume little oil and export most of their production, they are part of [OPEC](166346.md). Venezuela had a big drop in production after 2015 (when it produced 2.5 million barrels a day), falling in 2016 to 2.2 million, in 2017 to 2 million, in 2018 to 1.4 million and in 2019 to 877 thousand, due to lack of investment.[^cite_note-eia.gov-148]

In natural gas production, in 2018, Argentina produced 1,524 bcf (billions of cubic feet), Mexico produced 999, Venezuela 946, Brazil 877, Bolivia 617, Peru 451, Colombia 379.[^cite_note-149]

In coal production, the continent had three of the 30 largest world producers in 2018: Colombia (12th), Mexico (24th) and Brazil (27th).[^cite_note-bp.com-150]

### Manufacturing



[Braskem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braskem), the largest Brazilian chemical industry



[EMS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EMS_\(pharmaceuticals\)), the largest Brazilian pharmaceutical industry



Steel production in [San Nicolás](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Nicol%C3%A1s_de_los_Arroyos), Argentina.

The [World Bank](36752.md) annually lists the top manufacturing countries by total manufacturing value. According to the 2019 list:

- Mexico had the twelfth most valuable industry in the world (US\$217.8 billion)

- Brazil the thirteenth largest (US\$173.6 billion)

- Venezuela the thirtieth largest (US\$58.2 billion, however, it depends on oil to reach this value)

- Argentina the 31st largest (US\$57.7 billion)

- Colombia the 46th largest (US\$35.4 billion)

- Peru the 50th largest (US\$28.7 billion)

- Chile the 51st largest (US\$28.3 billion).

  [^cite_note-151]

In Latin America, few countries stand out in industrial activity: Brazil, Argentina, Mexico and, less prominently, Chile. Begun late, the industrialization of these countries received a great boost from World War II: this prevented the countries at war from buying the products they were used to importing and exporting what they produced. At that time, benefiting from the abundant local raw material, the low wages paid to the labor force and a certain specialization brought by immigrants, countries such as Brazil, Mexico and Argentina, as well as Venezuela, Chile, Colombia and Peru, were able to implement important industrial parks. In general, in these countries there are industries that require little capital and simple technology for their installation, such as the food processing and textile industries. The basic industries (steel, etc.) also stand out, as well as the metallurgical and mechanical industries.[^cite_note-152]

The industrial parks of Brazil, Mexico, Argentina and Chile, however, present much greater diversity and sophistication, producing advanced technology items. In the rest of Latin American countries, mainly in Central America, the processing industries of primary products for export predominate.

In the [food industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_industry), in 2019, Brazil was the second largest exporter of processed foods in the world.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155] In 2016, the country was the second largest producer of [pulp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulp_\(paper\)) in the world and the eighth largest producer of paper.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] In the [footwear industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Footwear_industry), in 2019, Brazil ranked fourth among world producers.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] In 2019, the country was the eighth largest producer of [vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicles) and the ninth largest producer of steel in the world.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165] In 2018, the [chemical industry](58721.md) of Brazil was the eighth largest in the world.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] In the [textile industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry), Brazil, although it was among the five largest world producers in 2013, is very little integrated into world trade.[^cite_note-169] In the aviation sector, Brazil has [Embraer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embraer), the third largest aircraft manufacturer in the world, behind [Boeing](18933266.md) and [Airbus](26220236.md).

### Infrastructure



[Panama Canal expansion project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Canal_expansion_project); New *Agua Clara* locks (Atlantic side)



[General Rafael Urdaneta Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Rafael_Urdaneta_Bridge)

Transport in Latin America is carried out primarily by road, the most developed mode in the region. There is also considerable [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port) and [airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport) infrastructure. The railway and [fluvial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluvial) sector, although it has potential, is usually treated in a secondary way.

Brazil has more than 1,700,000 km (1,100,000 mi) of [roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads), of which 215,000 km (134,000 mi) are paved, and about 14,000 km (8,700 mi) are [divided highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_carriageway). The two most important highways in the country are [BR-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-101) and [BR-116](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-116).[^cite_note-Anuário_CNT_do_transporte_2018-170] Argentina has more than 600,000 km (370,000 mi) of roads, of which about 70,000 km (43,000 mi) are paved, and about 2,500 km (1,600 mi) are divided highways. The three most important highways in the country are [Route 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Route_9_\(Argentina\)), [Route 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Route_7_\(Argentina\)) and [Route 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Route_14_\(Argentina\)).[^cite_note-Anuário_CNT_do_transporte_2018-170] Colombia has about 210,000 km (130,000 mi) of roads, and about 2,300 km (1,400 mi) are divided highways.[^cite_note-171] Chile has about 82,000 km (51,000 mi) of roads, 20,000 km (12,000 mi) of which are paved, and about 2,000 km (1,200 mi) are divided highways. The most important highway in the country is the [Route 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chile_Route_5) ([Pan-American Highway](301108.md))[^cite_note-172] These 4 countries have the most developed road infrastructure and the largest number of double-lane highways in South America.

The roadway network in Mexico has an extent of 366,095 km (227,481 mi),[^cite_note-cis-173] of which 116,802 km (72,577 mi) are paved,[^cite_note-sctroad-174][^cite_note-175] Of these, 10,474 km (6,508 mi) are multi-lane [expressways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-access_highway): 9,544 km (5,930 mi) are four-lane highways and the rest have 6 or more lanes.[^cite_note-sctroad-174]

&#x20;Due to the [Andes Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andes_Mountains), [Amazon River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazon_River) and [Amazon Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazon_Forest), there have always been difficulties in implementing transcontinental or bioceanic highways. Practically the only route that existed was the one that connected Brazil to Buenos Aires, in Argentina and later to Santiago, in Chile. However, in recent years, with the combined effort of countries, new routes have started to emerge, such as Brazil-Peru ([Interoceanic Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interoceanic_Highway)), and a new highway between Brazil, Paraguay, northern Argentina and northern Chile ([Bioceanic Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bi-Oceanic_railway)).



Port of Itajaí, Santa Catarina, Brazil

There are more than 2,000 airports in Brazil. The country has the second largest number of airports in the world, behind only the United States. [São Paulo International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo/Guarulhos_International_Airport), located in the Metropolitan Region of São Paulo, is the largest and busiest in the country – the airport connects São Paulo to practically all major cities around the world. Brazil has 44 international airports, such as those in [Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro/Gale%C3%A3o_International_Airport), [Brasília](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bras%C3%ADlia_International_Airport), [Belo Horizonte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belo_Horizonte_International_Airport), [Porto Alegre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salgado_Filho_International_Airport), [Florianópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herc%C3%ADlio_Luz_International_Airport), [Cuiabá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marechal_Rondon_International_Airport), [Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_International_Airport), [Recife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recife/Guararapes%E2%80%93Gilberto_Freyre_International_Airport), [Fortaleza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortaleza_Airport), [Belém](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bel%C3%A9m/Val-de-Cans_International_Airport) and [Manaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduardo_Gomes_International_Airport), among others. Argentina has important international airports such as [Buenos Aires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministro_Pistarini_International_Airport), [Cordoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingeniero_Aeron%C3%A1utico_Ambrosio_L.V._Taravella_International_Airport), [Bariloche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Carlos_de_Bariloche_Airport), [Mendoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_Francisco_Gabrielli_International_Airport), [Salta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mart%C3%ADn_Miguel_de_G%C3%BCemes_International_Airport), [Puerto Iguazú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cataratas_del_Iguaz%C3%BA_International_Airport), [Neuquén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidente_Per%C3%B3n_International_Airport) and [Ushuaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ushuaia_%E2%80%93_Malvinas_Argentinas_International_Airport), among others. Chile has important international airports such as [Santiago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Merino_Ben%C3%ADtez_International_Airport), [Antofagasta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Sabella_G%C3%A1lvez_International_Airport), [Puerto Montt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Tepual_Airport), [Punta Arenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidente_Carlos_Ib%C3%A1%C3%B1ez_del_Campo_International_Airport) and [Iquique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Aracena_International_Airport), among others. Colombia has important international airports such as [Bogotá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Dorado_International_Airport), [Medellín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mar%C3%ADa_C%C3%B3rdova_International_Airport), [Cartagena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_N%C3%BA%C3%B1ez_International_Airport), [Cali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_Bonilla_Arag%C3%B3n_International_Airport) and [Barranquilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Cortissoz_International_Airport), among others. Peru has important international airports such as [Lima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Ch%C3%A1vez_International_Airport), [Cuzco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Velasco_Astete_International_Airport) and [Arequipa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodr%C3%ADguez_Ball%C3%B3n_International_Airport). Other important airports are those in the capitals of Uruguay ([Montevideo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrasco_International_Airport)), Paraguay ([Asunción](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvio_Pettirossi_International_Airport)), Bolivia ([La Paz](2614073.md)) and Ecuador ([Quito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariscal_Sucre_International_Airport)). The 10 busiest airports in South America in 2017 were: São Paulo-Guarulhos (Brazil), Bogotá (Colombia), São Paulo-Congonhas (Brazil), Santiago (Chile), Lima (Peru), Brasília (Brazil), Rio de Janeiro (Brazil), Buenos Aires-Aeroparque (Argentina), Buenos Aires-Ezeiza (Argentina), and Minas Gerais (Brazil).[^cite_note-panrotas.com.br-176]

There are 1,580 airports in Mexico, the fourth-largest number of airports by country in the world.[^cite_note-177] The seven largest airports—which absorb 90% of air travel—are (in order of air traffic): [Mexico City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico_City_International_Airport), [Cancún](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canc%C3%BAn_International_Airport), [Guadalajara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara_International_Airport), [Monterrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monterrey_International_Airport), [Tijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuana_International_Airport), [Acapulco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acapulco_International_Airport), and [Puerto Vallarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licenciado_Gustavo_D%C3%ADaz_Ordaz_International_Airport).[^cite_note-mcx-178] Considering all of Latin America, the 10 busiest airports in 2024 were: Bogotá (Colombia), Mexico City (Mexico), São Paulo-Guarulhos (Brazil), Cancún (Mexico), Santiago (Chile), Lima (Peru), São Paulo-Congonhas (Brazil), Tocumen (Panama), Guadalajara (Mexico) and Brasilia (Brazil).[^cite_note-179]

Brazil has some of the busiest ports in South America, such as [Port of Santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Santos), [Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Port of Paranaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Paranagu%C3%A1), [Port of Itajaí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Itaja%C3%AD), [Port of Rio Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_Grande), [Port of São Francisco do Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_S%C3%A3o_Francisco_do_Sul) and [Suape Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suape_Port). Argentina has ports such as [Port of Buenos Aires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Buenos_Aires) and [Port of Rosario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rosario). Chile has important ports in [Valparaíso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valpara%C3%ADso), [Caldera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caldera,_Chile), [Mejillones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mejillones), [Antofagasta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antofagasta), [Iquique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iquique), [Arica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arica) and [Puerto Montt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Montt). Colombia has important ports such as [Buenaventura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buenaventura,_Valle_del_Cauca), [Cartagena Container Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartagena_Container_Terminal) and [Puerto Bolivar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Bolivar,_Colombia). Peru has important ports in [Callao](294857.md), [Ilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilo,_Peru) and [Matarani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matarani). The 15 busiest ports in South America are: Port of Santos (Brazil), Port of Bahia de Cartagena (Colombia), Callao (Peru), Guayaquil (Ecuador), Buenos Aires (Argentina), San Antonio (Chile), Buenaventura (Colombia), Itajaí (Brazil), Valparaíso (Chile), Montevideo (Uruguay), Paranaguá (Brazil), Rio Grande (Brazil), São Francisco do Sul (Brazil), Manaus (Brazil) and Coronel (Chile).[^cite_note-cepal.org-180]

The four major seaports concentrating around 60% of the merchandise traffic in Mexico are [Altamira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altamira,_Tamaulipas) and [Veracruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veracruz,_Veracruz) in the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md), and [Manzanillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzanillo,_Colima) and [Lázaro Cárdenas](2313626.md) in the Pacific Ocean. Considering all of Latin America, the 10 largest ports in terms of movement are: Colon (Panama), Santos (Brazil), Manzanillo (Mexico), Bahia de Cartagena (Colombia), Pacifico (Panama), Callao (Peru), Guayaquil (Ecuador), Buenos Aires (Argentina), San Antonio (Chile) and Buenaventura (Colombia).[^cite_note-cepal.org-180]

The [Brazilian railway network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Brazil) has an extension of about 30,000 kilometers. It is basically used for transporting ores.[^cite_note-181] The [Argentine rail network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Argentina), with 47,000 km of tracks, was one of the largest in the world and continues to be the most extensive in Latin America. It came to have about 100,000 km of rails, but the lifting of tracks and the emphasis placed on motor transport gradually reduced it. It has four different trails and international connections with Paraguay, Bolivia, Chile, Brazil and Uruguay. [Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Chile) has almost 7,000 km of railways, with connections to Argentina, Bolivia and Peru. [Colombia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Colombia) has only about 3,500 km of railways.[^cite_note-182]

Among the main Brazilian [waterways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterways), two stand out: Hidrovia Tietê-Paraná (which has a length of 2,400 km, 1,600 on the Paraná River and 800 km on the Tietê River, draining agricultural production from the states of Mato Grosso, Mato Grosso do Sul, Goiás and part of Rondônia, Tocantins and Minas General) and Hidrovia do Solimões-Amazonas (it has two sections: Solimões, which extends from Tabatinga to Manaus, with approximately 1600 km, and Amazonas, which extends from Manaus to Belém, with 1650 km. Almost entirely passenger transport from the Amazon plain is done by this waterway, in addition to practically all cargo transportation that is directed to the major regional centers of Belém and Manaus). In Brazil, this transport is still underutilized: the most important waterway stretches, from an economic point of view, are found in the Southeast and South of the country. Its full use still depends on the construction of locks, major dredging works and, mainly, of ports that allow intermodal integration. In Argentina, the waterway network is made up of the La Plata, Paraná, Paraguay and Uruguay rivers. The main river ports are [Zárate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z%C3%A1rate,_Buenos_Aires) and [Campana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campana_Partido). The port of Buenos Aires is historically the first in individual importance, but the area known as Up-River, which stretches along 67 km of the Santa Fé portion of the Paraná River, brings together 17 ports that concentrate 50% of the total exports of the country.

### Energy

As of 2023, Latin America and the Caribbean generates 60% of its electricity from [renewable energy](25784.md) – double the global average of 30%. Despite this, fossil fuels still play a substantial role, especially in transportation and industry, with oil and gas constituting a notable portion. Approximately two-thirds of the region's [energy mix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_mix) comes from fossil fuels,[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] Of the region's total energy production, 43% is [hydroelectric](381399.md), 8% [wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power) and 6% is [solar](13690575.md).[^cite_note-185]

#### Brazil

Main articles: [Energy policy of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Brazil) and [Renewable energy in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_Brazil)



[Itaipu Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itaipu_Dam) in Paraná



[Wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power) in [Parnaíba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parna%C3%ADba)



[Angra Nuclear Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angra_Nuclear_Power_Plant) in [Angra dos Reis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angra_dos_Reis), Rio de Janeiro



[Pirapora Solar Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirapora_Solar_Complex), the largest in Brazil and Latin America with a capacity of 321 MW

The Brazilian government has undertaken an ambitious program to reduce dependence on imported petroleum. Imports previously accounted for more than 70% of the country's oil needs but Brazil became self-sufficient in oil in 2006–2007. Brazil was the 10th largest oil producer in the world in 2019, with 2.8 million barrels / day. Production manages to supply the country's demand.[^cite_note-eia.gov-148] In the beginning of 2020, in the production of [oil](23195.md) and natural gas, the country exceeded 4 million [barrels of oil equivalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrels_of_oil_equivalent) per day, for the first time. In January this year, 3.168 million barrels of oil per day and 138.753 million cubic meters of natural gas were extracted.[^cite_note-186]

Brazil is one of the main world producers of [hydroelectric power](381399.md). In 2019, Brazil had 217 hydroelectric plants in operation, with an installed capacity of 98,581 MW, 60.16% of the country's energy generation.[^cite_note-187] In the total generation of electricity, in 2019 Brazil reached 170,000 megawatts of installed capacity, more than 75% from renewable sources (the majority, hydroelectric).[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

In 2013, the [Southeast Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Region,_Brazil) used about 50% of the load of the National Integrated System (SIN), being the main energy consuming region in the country. The region's installed electricity generation capacity totaled almost 42,500 MW, which represented about a third of Brazil's generation capacity. The [hydroelectric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric) generation represented 58% of the region's installed capacity, with the remaining 42% corresponding basically to the [thermoelectric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermoelectric) generation. São Paulo accounted for 40% of this capacity; Minas Gerais by about 25%; Rio de Janeiro by 13.3%; and Espírito Santo accounted for the rest. The [South Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Region,_Brazil) owns the [Itaipu Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itaipu_Dam), which was the largest hydroelectric plant in the world for several years, until the inauguration of [Three Gorges Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Gorges_Dam) in China. It remains the second largest operating hydroelectric in the world. Brazil is the co-owner of the Itaipu Plant with Paraguay: the dam is located on the [Paraná River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paran%C3%A1_River), located on the border between countries. It has an installed generation capacity of 14 GW for 20 generating units of 700 MW each. [North Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Region,_Brazil) has large hydroelectric plants, such as [Belo Monte Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belo_Monte_Dam) and [Tucuruí Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucuru%C3%AD_Dam), which produce much of the national energy. Brazil's hydroelectric potential has not yet been fully exploited, so the country still has the capacity to build several renewable energy plants in its territory.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191]

As of July 2022, according to ONS, total installed capacity of [wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power) was 22 GW, with average [capacity factor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capacity_factor) of 58%.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] While the world average wind production capacity factors is 24.7%, there are areas in Northern Brazil, specially in Bahia State, where some wind farms record with average capacity factors over 60%;[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-UNESCO-2015-195] the average capacity factor in the [Northeast Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Region,_Brazil) is 45% in the coast and 49% in the interior.[^cite_note-196] In 2019, wind energy represented 9% of the energy generated in the country.[^cite_note-cbie.com.br-197] In 2019, it was estimated that the country had an estimated wind power generation potential of around 522 GW (this, only onshore), enough energy to meet three times the country's current demand.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199] In 2021 Brazil was the 7th country in the world in terms of installed wind power (21 GW),[^cite_note-RENEWABLE_CAPACITY_STATISTICS_2021-200][^cite_note-201] and the 4th largest producer of wind energy in the world (72 TWh), behind only China, USA and Germany.[^cite_note-Ritchie-2022-202][^cite_note-RENEWABLE_CAPACITY_STATISTICS_2021-200]

Nuclear energy accounts for about 4% of Brazil's electricity.[^cite_note-uic-203] The nuclear power generation monopoly is owned by [Eletronuclear (Eletrobrás Eletronuclear S/A)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eletronuclear), a wholly owned subsidiary of [Eletrobrás](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eletrobr%C3%A1s). Nuclear energy is produced by [two reactors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nuclear_reactors#Brazil) at [Angra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angra_Nuclear_Power_Plant). It is located at the Central Nuclear Almirante Álvaro Alberto (CNAAA) on the Praia de Itaorna in [Angra dos Reis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angra_dos_Reis), Rio de Janeiro. It consists of two [pressurized water reactors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pressurized_water_reactor), Angra I, with capacity of 657 MW, connected to the power grid in 1982, and Angra II, with capacity of 1,350 MW, connected in 2000. A third reactor, Angra III, with a projected output of 1,350 MW, is planned to be finished.[^cite_note-merco-204]

As of October 2022, according to ONS, total installed capacity of [photovoltaic solar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photovoltaic_solar) was 21 GW, with average [capacity factor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capacity_factor) of 23%.[^cite_note-205] Some of the most [irradiated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_irradiance) Brazilian States are MG ("Minas Gerais"), BA ("Bahia") and GO ("Goiás"), which have indeed world irradiation level records.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-UNESCO-2015-195][^cite_note-207] In 2019, solar power represented 1.27% of the energy generated in the country.[^cite_note-cbie.com.br-197] In 2021, Brazil was the 14th country in the world in terms of installed solar power (13 GW),[^cite_note-208] and the 11th largest producer of solar energy in the world (16.8 TWh).[^cite_note-Ritchie-2022-202]

In 2020, Brazil was the 2nd largest country in the world in the production of energy through [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass) (energy production from solid biofuels and renewable waste), with 15,2 GW installed.[^cite_note-209]

#### Other countries

After Brazil, Mexico is the country in Latin America that most stands out in energy production. In 2020, the country was the 14th largest petroleum producer in the world, and in 2018 it was the 12th largest exporter. In natural gas, the country was, in 2015, the 21st largest producer in the world, and in 2007 it was the 29th largest exporter. Mexico was also the world's 24th largest producer of coal in 2018. In renewable energies, in 2020, the country ranked 14th in the world in terms of installed wind energy (8.1 GW), 20th in the world in terms of installed solar energy (5.6 GW) and 19th in the world in terms of installed hydroelectric power (12.6 GW). In third place, Colombia stands out: In 2020, the country was the 20th largest petroleum producer in the world, and in 2015 it was the 19th largest exporter. In natural gas, the country was, in 2015, the 40th largest producer in the world. Colombia's biggest highlight is in coal, where the country was, in 2018, the world's 12th largest producer and the 5th largest exporter. In renewable energies, in 2020, the country ranked 45th in the world in terms of installed wind energy (0.5 GW), 76th in the world in terms of installed solar energy (0.1 GW) and 20th in the world in terms of installed hydroelectric power (12.6 GW).

Venezuela, which was one of the world's largest oil producers (about 2.5 million barrels/day in 2015) and one of the largest exporters, due to its political problems, has had its production drastically reduced in recent years: in 2016, it dropped to 2.2 million, in 2017 to 2 million, in 2018 to 1.4 million and in 2019 to 877 thousand, reaching only 300,000 barrels/day at a given point. The country also stands out in hydroelectricity, where it was the 14th country in the world in terms of installed capacity in 2020 (16,5 GW).



[YPF](715706.md) [headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repsol-YPF_Tower) in Buenos Aires, Argentina.

Argentina was, in 2017, the 18th largest producer in the world, and the largest producer in Latin America, of natural gas, in addition to being the 28th largest oil producer; although the country has the Vaca Muerta field, which holds close to 16 billion barrels of technically recoverable shale oil, and is the second largest shale natural gas deposit in the world, the country lacks the capacity to exploit the deposit: it is necessary capital, technology and knowledge that can only come from offshore energy companies, who view Argentina and its erratic economic policies with considerable suspicion, not wanting to invest in the country. In renewable energies, in 2020, the country ranked 27th in the world in terms of installed wind energy (2.6 GW), 42nd in the world in terms of installed solar energy (0.7 GW) and 21st in the world in terms of installed hydroelectric power (11.3 GW). The country has great future potential for the production of wind energy in the Patagonia region. Chile, although currently not a major energy producer, has great future potential for solar energy production in the Atacama Desert region.

Paraguay stands out today in hydroelectric production thanks to the Itaipu Power Plant. Trinidad and Tobago and Bolivia stand out in the production of natural gas, where they were, respectively, the 20th and 31st largest in the world in 2015. Ecuador, because it consumes little energy, is part of OPEC and was the 27th largest oil producer in the world in 2020, being the 22nd largest exporter in 2014.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212][^cite_note-bp.com-150][^cite_note-RENEWABLE_CAPACITY_STATISTICS_2021-200]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Latin America and the Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Latin_America_and_the_Caribbean)



Aerial view of [Cancún](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canc%C3%BAn). Mexico is the most visited country in Latin America and 6th in the world.

Income from tourism is key to the economy of several Latin American countries.[^cite_note-213] Mexico is the only Latin American country to be ranked in the top 10 worldwide in the number of tourist visits. It received by far the largest number of international tourists, with 39.3 million visitors in 2017, followed by Argentina, with 6.7 million; then Brazil, with 6.6 million; Chile, with 6.5 million; Dominican Republic, with 6.2 million; Cuba with 4.3 million; Peru and Colombia with 4.0 million. The [World Tourism Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_Organization) reports the following destinations as the top six tourism earners for the year 2017: Mexico, with US\$21,333 million; the Dominican Republic, with US\$7,178 million; Brazil, with US\$6,024 million; Colombia, with US\$4,773 million; Argentina, with US\$4,687 million; and Panama, with US\$4,258 million.[^cite_note-WTO_Tourism_Highlights_2018_Edition-214]

### Trade blocs



[Rafael Correa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Correa), [Evo Morales](284163.md), [Néstor Kirchner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C3%A9stor_Kirchner), [Cristina Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristina_Fern%C3%A1ndez), [Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](99328.md), [Nicanor Duarte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicanor_Duarte), and [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) at the signing of the founding charter of the [Bank of the South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_the_South)

| --- | --- |

The major [trade blocs](307255.md) (or [agreements](217089.md)) in the region are the [Pacific Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Alliance) and [Mercosur](25869317.md). Minor blocs or trade agreements are the [G3 Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G3_Free_Trade_Agreement), the [Dominican Republic – Central America Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Republic_%E2%80%93_Central_America_Free_Trade_Agreement) (DR-CAFTA), the [Caribbean Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean_Community) (CARICOM) and the [Andean Community of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andean_Community_of_Nations) (CAN). However, major reconfigurations are taking place along opposing approaches to integration and trade; Venezuela has officially withdrawn from both the CAN and G3 and it has been formally admitted into the Mercosur (pending ratification from the Paraguayan legislature). The president-elect of Ecuador has manifested his intentions of following the same path. This bloc nominally opposes any Free Trade Agreement (FTA) with the United States, although Uruguay has manifested its intention otherwise. Chile, Peru, Colombia and Mexico are the only four Latin American nations that have an FTA with the United States and Canada, both members of the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement) (NAFTA).

#### China

China's economic influence in Latin America increased substantially in the 21st century. Imports from China valued \$8.3 billion in 2000, but by 2022 its value was \$450 billion and had grown to be the largest trading partner of South America, as well as the second-largest for the broader Latin America.[^cite_note-Council_on_Foreign_Relations-217] In particular, many of the investments are related to the [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative) or energy. China has also provided loans to several Latin American countries; this has raised concerns about the possibility of debt traps."[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-Council_on_Foreign_Relations-217] Specifically, Venezuela, Brazil, Ecuador, and Argentina received the most loans from China during 2005–2016.[^cite_note-220]

## Inequality

Main article: [Wealth inequality in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_inequality_in_Latin_America)



Slums on the outskirts of a wealthy urban area in [São Paulo](390875.md), Brazil.

[Wealth inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_inequality) in Latin America remains a serious issue despite strong economic growth and improved social indicators. A report released in 2013 by the [UN Department of Economic and Social Affairs](13372331.md) entitled *Inequality Matters: Report of the World Social Situation*, observed that: 'Declines in the wage share have been attributed to the impact of labour-saving technological change and to a general weakening of labour market regulations and institutions.'[^cite_note-UNESCO-2015-195] Such declines are likely to disproportionately affect individuals in the middle and bottom of the [income distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_distribution), as they rely mostly on wages for income. In addition, the report noted that 'highly-unequal land distribution has created social and political tensions and is a source of [economic inefficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_efficiency), as small landholders frequently lack access to credit and other resources to increase [productivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Productivity), while big owners may not have had enough incentive to do so.[^cite_note-UNESCO-2015-195][^cite_note-221]

According to the United Nations [ECLAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ECLAC), Latin America is the most unequal region in the world.[^cite_note-222] In 2025, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* said that Latin America's "tax and welfare systems are shockingly bad at reducing inequality."[^cite_note-223]

### History of inequality

Main article: [Spain in the 17th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain_in_the_17th_century)



[Spanish Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition)'s *[auto-da-fé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto-da-f%C3%A9)* held in the [Plaza Mayor, Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Mayor,_Madrid) in 1680

Spanish society in 17th century [Habsburg Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_Spain) was extremely inegalitarian. The nobility, being wealthier than ordinary people, also had the privilege of being exempt from taxes. Spanish society associated social status with leisure and thus work was undignified for nobles. Even wealthy merchants invested in land, titles, and juros. Two acceptable careers for the nobility were the church and education. In 1620, there were 100,000 Spaniards in the clergy, by the late 17th century there were 150,000. Many Spaniards spent long years in universities, taking advantage of the increase in its numbers. By 1660, there were about 200,000 Spaniards in the clergy and the Church owned 20% of all the land in Spain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamen2005-224]

Inequality in Latin America has deep historical roots in the [Latin European racially based Casta system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casta)[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] instituted in Latin America during colonial times that has been difficult to eradicate because of the differences between initial endowments and opportunities among social groups have constrained the poorest's [social mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_mobility), thus causing poverty to transmit from generation to generation, and become a vicious cycle. Inequality has been reproduced and transmitted through generations because Latin American political systems allow a differentiated access on the influence that social groups have in the decision-making process, and it responds in different ways to the least favored groups that have less political representation and capacity of pressure.[^cite_note-227] Recent [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation) also plays a role as not everyone is [equally capable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equity_\(economics\)) of taking advantage of its benefits.[^cite_note-228] Differences in opportunities and endowments tend to be based on [race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism), ethnicity, rurality, and [gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_inequality). Because inequality in gender and location are near-universal, race and ethnicity play a larger, more integral role in discriminatory practices in Latin America. The differences have a strong impact on the distribution of income, capital and political standing.

One indicator of inequality is access to and quality of education. During the first phase of [globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization) in Latin America, educational inequality was on the rise, peaking around the end of the 19th century. In comparison with other [developing regions](78449.md), Latin America then had the highest level of [educational inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_inequality), which is certainly a contributing factor for its current general high inequality. During the 20th century, however, educational inequality started decreasing.[^cite_note-229]

### Race and ethnicity

Main articles: [Ethnic groups in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Latin_America) and [Race and ethnicity in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_ethnicity_in_Latin_America)



Eighteenth-century Mexican Casta painting showing 16 castas hierarchically arranged. [Ignacio Maria Barreda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignacio_Maria_Barreda), 1777. Real Academia Española de la Lengua, Madrid.

| icon | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Latin_America) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(April 2022)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Latin American populations are diverse, with descendants of the Indigenous peoples, Europeans, Africans initially brought as slaves, and Asians, as well as new immigrants.

Mixing of groups was a fact of life at contact of the Old World and the New, but colonial regimes established legal and social discrimination against non-white populations simply on the basis of perceived ethnicity and skin color. Social class was usually linked to a person's racial category, with European-born Spaniards and Portuguese on top. During the colonial era, with a dearth initially of European women, European men and Indigenous women and African women produced what were considered mixed-race children. In Spanish America, the so-called *Sociedad de [castas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castas)* or *Sistema de castas* was constructed by white elites to try to rationalize the processes at work. In the sixteenth century the Spanish crown sought to protect Indigenous populations from exploitation by white elites for their labor and land. The crown created th&#x65;*&#x20;[ República de indios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rep%C3%BAblica_de_indios?action=edit\&redlink=1)* to paternalistically govern and protect Indigenous peoples. It also created the *República de Españoles*, which included not only European whites, but all non-Indigenous peoples, such as Black, mulattoes, and mixed-race castas who were not dwelling in Indigenous communities. In the religious sphere, the Indigenous were deemed perpetual neophytes in the Catholic faith, which meant Indigenous men were not eligible to be ordained as Catholic priests; however, Indigenous were also excluded from the jurisdiction of the [Inquisition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquisition). Catholics saw military conquest and religious conquest as two parts of the assimilation of Indigenous populations, suppressing Indigenous religious practices and eliminating the Indigenous priesthood. Some worship continued underground. Jews and other non-Catholics, such as Protestants (all called "Lutherans") were banned from settling and were subject to the Inquisition.



Map of ethno-racial distribution by country.

Considerable mixing of populations occurred in cities, while the countryside was largely Indigenous. At independence in the early nineteenth century, in many places in Spanish America formal racial and legal distinctions disappeared, although slavery was not uniformly abolished. Significant black populations exist in Brazil and Spanish Caribbean islands such as Cuba and Puerto Rico and the circum-Caribbean mainland (Venezuela, Colombia, Panama), as long as in the southern part of South America and Central America (Honduras, Costa Rica, Nicaragua, Ecuador, and Peru) a legacy of their use in plantations. All these areas also have significant white populations. In Brazil, coastal Indigenous peoples largely died out in the early sixteenth century, with Indigenous populations surviving far from cities, sugar plantations, and other European enterprises. Many mixed-race people in much of Latin America are tri-racial, usually of European, African, and Indigenous blood, where European (mostly Spanish/Portuguese) tends to be the strongest of the three. In most of Brazil and the Spanish Caribbean, the average ancestral mix is European and African blood, with much smaller amounts of indigenous blood. While the opposite is true in many mainland Spanish-speaking Latin American countries like Venezuela, Colombia, and Panama, where the average ancestral mix is of European and indigenous blood, with smaller amounts of African. But in Mexico, and other places in northern Central America and southern South America, mixed race people tend to be completely of European and indigenous blood.

Dominican Republic, Puerto Rico, Cuba, and Brazil have dominant Mulatto/Triracial populations ("Pardo" in Brazil). More specifically, in Brazil and Cuba, there is equally large white populations and smaller black populations, while Dominican Republic and Puerto Rico are more Mulatto/Triracial dominated, with significant black and white minorities. Parts of Central America and northern South America are more diverse in that they are predominantly made up of Mestizos and Whites but also have large numbers of Mulattos, Black, and Indigenous people, especially Colombia, Venezuela, and Panama.



[Italian immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Argentines) arriving in [Buenos Aires](255919.md), during the [great European immigration wave to Argentina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_European_immigration_wave_to_Argentina).

The [Southern Cone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cone) region—encompassing [Argentina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentine_Primera_Divisi%C3%B3n), Uruguay, and Chile—is predominantly [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Latin_Americans) due to the massive [European immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_immigration_to_the_Americas) that occurred from the late 19th century to the mid-20th century.[^cite_note-242] The rest of Latin America, including Mexico, northern Central America—Guatemala, El Salvador, Honduras— and central South America—Peru, Ecuador, Bolivia, Paraguay—are dominated by mestizos but also have large white and indigenous minorities. Black people make up the majority of the French Caribbean, but it is generally not considered part of Latin America. In the nineteenth century, a number of Latin American countries sought immigrants from Europe and Asia. With the abolition of black slavery in 1888, the Brazilian monarchy fell in 1889. By then, another source of cheap labor to work on coffee plantations was found in Japan. Chinese male immigrants arrived in Cuba, Mexico, Peru and elsewhere. With political turmoil in Europe during the mid-nineteenth century and widespread poverty, Germans, Spaniards, and Italians immigrated to Latin America in large numbers, welcomed by Latin American governments both as a source of labor as well as a way to increase the size of their white populations. In Argentina, many Afro-Argentines married Euro-Argentines.[^cite_note-243]

In twentieth-century Brazil, sociologist [Gilberto Freyre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilberto_Freyre) proposed that Brazil was a "[racial democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_democracy)", with less discrimination against black people than in the United States.[^cite_note-244] Even if a system of legal racial segregation was never implemented in Latin America, unlike the United States, subsequent research has shown that in Brazil there's discrimination against darker citizens, and that whites remain the elites in the country.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246] In Mexico, the mestizo population was considered the true embodiment of "[the cosmic race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_raza_c%C3%B3smica)", according to Mexican intellectual [José Vasconcelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Vasconcelos), thus erasing other populations. There was considerable discrimination against Asians, with calls for the expulsion of [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_immigration_to_Mexico) in northern Mexico during the [Mexican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Revolution) (1910–1920) and racially motivated [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torre%C3%B3n_massacre). In a number of Latin American countries, Indigenous groups have organized explicitly as Indigenous, to claim human rights and influence political power. With the passage of anti-colonial resolutions in the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) and the signing of resolutions for Indigenous rights, the Indigenous are able to act to guarantee their existence within nation-states with legal [standing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing).

### Language

| --- | --- |



Trilingual sign in Spanish, Italian and [Piedmontese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piedmontese_language) in [San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco,_Argentina), [Córdoba Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba_Province,_Argentina), Argentina.



Most widely spoken Native languages in Latin America:\
**Quechua**, **Guaraní**, **Aymara**, **Náhuatl**, **Mayan Languages**, **Mapudungun**

Spanish is the predominant language of Latin America. It is spoken as a first language by about 60% of the population. Portuguese is spoken by about 30%, and about 10% speak other languages such as [Quechua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quechuan_languages), [Mayan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayan_languages), [Guaraní](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guarani_language), [Aymara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aymara_language), [Nahuatl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahuatl), English, French, [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) and Italian. Spanish is the official language of most of the countries and territories on the Latin American mainland, as well as in Cuba, Puerto Rico (where it is co-official with English), and the Dominican Republic. Portuguese is spoken mostly in Brazil, the largest and most populous country in the region. French is spoken in Haiti and in the French [overseas departments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_department) of [Guadeloupe](12343.md), Martinique, and [French Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Guiana). It is also spoken by some [Panamanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panamanians) of Afro-[Antillean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antilles) descent. Dutch is the official language in Suriname, Aruba, [Curaçao](65205.md) and [Bonaire](108737.md). As Dutch is a [Germanic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_languages), the territories are not necessarily considered part of Latin America. However, the native and co-official language of Aruba, Bonaire, and Curaçao, is [Papiamento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papiamento), a [creole language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_language) largely based on Portuguese and Spanish that has had a considerable influence from Dutch and other [Portuguese-based creole languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese-based_creole_languages).[^cite_note-248]

[Amerindian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_languages_of_the_Americas) are widely spoken in Peru, Guatemala, Bolivia, Paraguay and Mexico, and to a lesser degree, in Panama, Ecuador, Brazil, Colombia, Venezuela, Argentina, and Chile. In other Latin American countries, the population of speakers of Indigenous languages tend to be very small or even non-existent, for example in Uruguay. Mexico is possibly contains more Indigenous languages than any other Latin American country, but the most-spoken Indigenous language there is Nahuatl.[^cite_note-249]

In Peru, [Quechua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quechua_languages) is an official language, alongside Spanish and other Indigenous languages in the areas where they predominate. In Ecuador, while [Quichua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quichua) holds no official status, it is a recognized language under the country's constitution; however, it is only spoken by a few groups in the country's highlands. In Bolivia, [Aymara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aymara_language), Quechua, and [Guaraní](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaran%C3%AD_language) hold official status alongside Spanish. Guaraní, like Spanish, is an official language of Paraguay, and is spoken by a majority of the population, which is, for the most part, bilingual, and it is co-official with Spanish in the Argentine province of [Corrientes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corrientes). In Nicaragua, Spanish is the official language, but on the country's Caribbean coast English and Indigenous languages such as [Miskito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miskito_language), [Sumo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumo_languages), and [Rama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rama_language) also hold official status. Colombia recognizes all Indigenous languages spoken within its territory as official, though fewer than 1% of its population are native speakers of these languages. [Nahuatl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahuatl) is one of the 62 Native languages spoken by Indigenous people in Mexico, which are officially recognized by the government as "national languages" along with Spanish.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251]

Other European languages spoken in Latin America include: English, by half of the current population in Puerto Rico, as well as in nearby countries that may or may not be considered Latin American, like Belize and Guyana, and spoken by descendants of British settlers in Argentina and Chile. German is spoken in southern Brazil, southern Chile, portions of Argentina, Venezuela and Paraguay; [Italian in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language_in_Brazil), Argentina, Venezuela, and Uruguay; [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language), [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language), and Russian in southern Brazil and Argentina; and [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_language), in southern Argentina.[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254] Non-European or Asian languages include Japanese in Brazil, Peru, Bolivia, and Paraguay, Korean in Brazil, Argentina, Paraguay, and Chile, Arabic in Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Venezuela, and Chile, and Chinese throughout South America. Countries like Venezuela, Argentina and Brazil have their own dialects or variations of German and Italian.

In several nations, especially in the Caribbean region, [creole languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_languages) are spoken. The most widely-spoken creole language in the combined Latin America and the Caribbean region is [Haitian Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Creole), the predominant language of Haiti, derived primarily from French and certain West African tongues, with [Amerindian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas), English, Portuguese, and Spanish influences as well. Creole languages of mainland Latin America, similarly, are derived from European languages and various African tongues. The aforementioned Papiamento, commonly spoken on the Dutch Caribbean [ABC Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_islands_\(Leeward_Antilles\)), is a [Portuguese-based creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese-based_creole_languages).

The [Garifuna language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garifuna_language) is spoken along the Caribbean coast in Honduras, Guatemala, Nicaragua, and Belize, mostly by the [Garifuna people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garifuna_people), a mixed-race [Zambo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambo) people who were the result of mixing between Indigenous Caribbeans and escaped Black slaves. Primarily an [Arawakan language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arawakan_languages), it has influences from Caribbean and European languages.

Archaeologists have deciphered over 15 pre-Columbian distinct writing systems from Mesoamerican societies. Ancient [Maya](18449273.md) had the most sophisticated textually written language, but since texts were largely confined to the religious and administrative elite, traditions were passed down orally. Oral traditions also prevailed in other major Indigenous groups including, but not limited to, the [Aztecs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztecs) and other Nahuatl speakers, [Quechua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quechua_people) and Aymara of the [Andean regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andean_world), the [Quiché](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K'iche'_people) of Central America, the [Tupi-Guaraní](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupi-Guaran%C3%AD) in today's Brazil, the [Guaraní](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaran%C3%AD_people) in Paraguay, and the [Mapuche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mapuche) in Chile.[^cite_note-255]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Latin_America)



The first [Mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_\(liturgy\)) in Brazil among the [Native Brazilians](55510.md) on 26 April 1500. Painting by [Victor Meirelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Meirelles) (1860).



The [Las Lajas Sanctuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Lajas_Sanctuary) in southern Colombia, Department of [Nariño](549702.md)



[Cathedral of Arequipa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_Cathedral_of_Arequipa), in Southern Peru



[Buenos Aires Metropolitan Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buenos_Aires_Metropolitan_Cathedral), where the remains of [General José de San Martín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn) lie, the [Libertador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertadores) of Argentina, Chile and Peru.

The vast majority of Latin Americans are Christians (90%),[^cite_note-256] mostly Roman Catholics belonging to the [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church).[^cite_note-257] About 70% of the Latin American population considers itself Catholic.[^cite_note-258] In 2012 Latin America constitutes in absolute terms the second [world's largest Christian population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_by_country), after Europe.[^cite_note-Survey1-259]

According to the detailed Pew multi-country survey in 2014, 69% of the Latin American population is Catholic and 19% is Protestant. Protestants are 26% of the population in Brazil and over 40% in much of Central America. More than half of these are converts from Roman Catholicism.[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-Religion_in_Latin_America-261]&#x20;
