### Family structure



The adolescent birth rate in women aged 10–19 years, 2016

[^cite_note-263]

Latin America has the highest rates of non-marital childbearing in the world, with 55–74% of all children in the region born to unmarried parents.[^cite_note-264] In most countries in this traditionally Catholic region, children born outside marriage are now the norm. In 2014, non-marital births were 74% in Colombia, 70% in Paraguay, 69% in Peru, 63% in the Dominican Republic, 58% in Argentina, and 55% in Mexico.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-sustaindemographicdividend.org-266][^cite_note-267] In Brazil, non-marital births increased to 65.8% in 2009, up from 56.2% in 2000.[^cite_note-268] In Chile, non-marital births increased to 70.7% in 2013, up from 48.3% in 2000.[^cite_note-269]

Latin America also has relatively high teenage pregnancy rates, much higher than North America, Europe, and much of Asia, but lower than sub-Saharan Africa.

### Migration

See also: [Latin American diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_diaspora)

The entire hemisphere was settled by migrants from Asia, Europe, and Africa. Indigenous Amerindian populations settled throughout the hemisphere before the arrival of Europeans in the late fifteenth and sixteenth centuries and the forced migration of slaves from Africa.

In the post-independence period, a number of Latin American countries sought to attract European immigrants as a source of labor as well as to deliberately change the proportions of racial and ethnic groups within their borders. Chile, Argentina, and Brazil actively recruited labor from Catholic southern Europe, where populations were poor and sought better economic opportunities. Many nineteenth-century immigrants went to the United States and Canada, but a significant number arrived in Latin America. Although Mexico tried to attract immigrants, it largely failed.[^cite_note-270] As black slavery was abolished in Brazil in 1888, coffee growers recruited Japanese migrants to work in coffee plantations. There is a significant population of Japanese descent in Brazil. Cuba and Peru recruited Chinese labor in the late nineteenth century. Some Chinese immigrants who were excluded from immigrating to the United States settled in northern Mexico. When the United States acquired its southwest by conquest in the [Mexican–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War), Latin American populations did not cross the border to the United States, the border crossed them.

In the twentieth century there have been several types of migration. One is the movement of rural populations within a given country to cities in search of work, causing many Latin American cities to grow significantly. Another is international movement of populations, often fleeing repression or war. Other international migration is for economic reasons, often unregulated or undocumented. Mexicans immigrated to the U.S. during the violence of the [Mexican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Revolution) (1910–1920)[^cite_note-271] and the religious [Cristero War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristero_War) (1926–29);[^cite_note-272] during World War II, Mexican men worked in the U.S. in the [bracero program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bracero_program). Economic migration from Mexico followed the crash of the Mexican economy in the 1980s.[^cite_note-273] Spanish refugees fled to Mexico following the fascist victory in the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War) (1936–38), with some 50,000 exiles finding refuge at the invitation of President [Lázaro Cárdenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A1zaro_C%C3%A1rdenas).[^cite_note-274] Following World War II a larger wave of refugees to Latin America, many of them Jews, settled in Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Cuba, and Venezuela. Some were only transiting through the region, but others stayed and created communities.[^cite_note-275] A number of Nazis escaped to Latin America, living under assumed names, in an attempt to avoid attention and prosecution.

In the aftermath of the Cuban Revolution, middle class and elite Cubans moved to the U.S., particularly to Florida. Some fled Chile for the U.S. and Europe after the 1973 military coup.[^cite_note-276] Colombians migrated to Spain and the United Kingdom during the region's political turmoil, compounded by the rise of narcotrafficking and [guerrilla warfare](12720.md).[^cite_note-277] During the Central American wars of the 1970s to the 1990s, many Salvadorans, Guatemalans, and Hondurans migrated to the U.S. to escape narcotrafficking, gangs, and poverty. As living conditions deteriorated in Venezuela under [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) and [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md), many left for neighboring Colombia and Ecuador. In the 1990s, economic stress in Ecuador during the [La Década Perdida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_D%C3%A9cada_Perdida) triggered considerable migration to Spain and to the U.S.[^cite_note-278]

Some Latin American countries seek to strengthen links between migrants and their states of origin, while promoting their integration in the receiving state. These emigrant policies focus on the rights, obligations, and opportunities for participation of emigrated citizens who already live outside the borders of the country of origin. Research on Latin America shows that the extension of policies towards migrants is linked to a focus on civil rights and state benefits that can positively influence integration in recipient countries. In addition, the tolerance of dual citizenship has spread more in Latin America than in any other region of the world.[^cite_note-279]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Latin_America)



World map indicating literacy rate by country in 2015 (2015 CIA *[World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Factbook)*). Grey = no data.



[Colegio Nacional de Buenos Aires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colegio_Nacional_de_Buenos_Aires), one of the most prestigious schools in Latin America. Its alumni includes two [Nobel](21201.md) laureates and four presidents of Argentina.

Despite significant progress, education access and school completion remains unequal in Latin America. The region has made great progress in educational coverage; almost all children attend primary school, and access to secondary education has increased considerably. Quality issues such as poor teaching methods, lack of appropriate equipment, and overcrowding exist throughout the region. These issues lead to adolescents dropping out of the educational system early. Most educational systems in the region have implemented various types of administrative and institutional reforms that have enabled reach for places and communities that had no access to education services in the early 1990s. School meal programs are also employed to expand access to education, and at least 23 countries in the Latin America and Caribbean region have large-scale school feeding activities, altogether reaching 88% of primary school-age children in the region.[^cite_note-280] Compared to prior generations, Latin American youth have seen an increase in their levels of education. On average, they have completed two more years of school than their parents.[^cite_note-Welti-281]

However, there are still 23 million children in the region between the ages of 4 and 17 outside of the formal education system. Estimates indicate that 30% of preschool age children (ages 4–5) do not attend school, and for the most vulnerable populations, the poor and rural, this proportion exceeds 40 percent. Among primary school age children (ages 6 to 12), attendance is almost universal; however, there is still a need to enroll five million more children in the primary education system. These children mostly live in remote areas, are Indigenous or Afro-descendants and live in extreme poverty.[^cite_note-Survey-282]

Among people between the ages of 13 and 17 years, only 80% are full-time students, and only 66% of these advance to secondary school. These percentages are lower among vulnerable population groups: only 75% of the poorest youth between the ages of 13 and 17 years attend school. Tertiary education has the lowest coverage, with only 70% of people between the ages of 18 and 25 years outside of the education system. Currently, more than half of low income or rural children fail to complete nine years of education.[^cite_note-Survey-282]

### Public health



Life expectancy and healthy life expectancy in Latin America in 2019

[^cite_note-who-286]

#### Health Development in Latin America

Latin America has made notable progress in developing healthcare throughout the recent decades, and this is mostly driven even by both domestic policy and international collaborations. Some developments that were used:

- The Pan American Health Organization. (*This section is an excerpt from [Pan American Health Organization](1246706.md)).* The **Pan American Health Organization** (**PAHO**) is a [specialized agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_specialized_agencies_of_the_United_Nations) of the United Nations (UN) in charge of international health cooperation in the Americas. This organization has played a crucial role in making efforts to combat communicable and noncommunicable diseases, strengthen health systems, and respond to emergencies and disasters.

- Public/Private Initiatives and Partnerships. An example that has been in place since 2015 is the Salud Mesoamérica Initiative (SMI).

  [^cite_note-287]

  &#x20;This research project that focuses on the inequalities in health outcomes and access in Central America. It is a model that supports the transformation of [health systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_Systems_Global) in each country, to bring coverage, access to healthcare, and health services among the poor in Mesoamérica, in particular women and children.

  [^cite_note-288]

#### Water



[Palace of Running Waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_Aguas_Corrientes), water pumping station and headquarters of [Obras Sanitarias de la Nación](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obras_Sanitarias_de_la_Naci%C3%B3n) soon after being inaugurated in Buenos Aires, Argentina.

This section is an excerpt from [Water supply and sanitation in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Latin_America).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Latin_America\&action=edit)]

[Water supply and sanitation in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Latin_America) is characterized by insufficient access and in many cases by poor service quality, with detrimental impacts on [public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health).[^cite_note-289] Water and [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation) services are provided by a vast array of mostly local service providers under an often fragmented policy and regulatory framework. Financing of water and sanitation remains a serious challenge.

#### Reproductive rights

This section is an excerpt from [Reproductive rights in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_rights_in_Latin_America).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Reproductive_rights_in_Latin_America\&action=edit)]

Latin America is home to some of the few countries of the world with a complete ban on abortion and minimal policies on [reproductive rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproductive_rights), but it also contains some of the most progressive reproductive rights movements in the world.[^cite_note-290] Debates on reproductive rights in the region occur over abortion, sexual autonomy, reproductive healthcare, and access to contraceptive measures.[^cite_note-291] Modern reproductive rights movements most notably include the [Green Wave (Marea Verde)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Wave_\(abortion_rights\)), which has led to much reproductive legislation reform.[^cite_note-Reproductive_rights_in_Latin_America_Amnesty_International-2019-292] [Cuba](5042481.md) has been a regional leader for more liberal reproductive laws, while other countries like [El Salvador](9356.md) and [Honduras](13394.md) have increased restrictions on reproductive rights.[^cite_note-Reproductive_rights_in_Latin_America_GAPD-293]

#### HIV/AIDS

This section is an excerpt from [HIV/AIDS in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America\&action=edit)]

HIV/AIDS has been a public health concern for Latin America due to a remaining prevalence of the disease.[^cite_note-HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America_\:0-294] As of 2024, approximately 2.5 million people in Latin America were living with HIV.[^cite_note-HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America_\:8-295] This is an increase from the 2018 estimation that 2.2 million people had HIV in Latin America and the Caribbean, making the HIV prevalence rate approximately 0.4% in Latin America.[^cite_note-HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America_\:0-294] In 2024, there were approximately 120,000 new HIV infections in Latin America, nearly all of which were among adults aged 15 and older.[^cite_note-HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America_\:8-295] That same year, the region experienced 27,000 AIDS-related deaths.[^cite_note-HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America_\:8-295] Overall, approximately 71% of all people living with HIV in Latin America are on an antiretroviral treatment (ART), though this regional treatment coverage percentage is lower for children (age 0-14) and pregnant women.[^cite_note-HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America_\:8-295]

Some demographic groups in Latin America have higher prevalence rates for HIV/ AIDS including men who have sex with men having a prevalence rate of 10.6%, and transgender women having one of the highest rates within the population with a prevalence rate of 17.7%.[^cite_note-HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America_\:1-296] Female sex workers and drug users also have higher prevalence for the disease than the general population (4.9% and 1%-49.7% respectively).[^cite_note-HIV/AIDS_in_Latin_America_\:1-296]

### Crime and violence

Main article: [Crime and violence in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_and_violence_in_Latin_America)



2012 map of countries by [homicide rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_intentional_homicide_rate). As of 2015, the Latin American countries with the highest rates were El Salvador (108.64 per 100,000 people), Honduras (63.75) and Venezuela (57.15). The countries with the lowest rates were Chile (3.59), Cuba (4.72) and Argentina (6.53).

Latin America has been cited by numerous sources to be the most dangerous region in the world.[^cite_note-297] Studies have shown that Latin America contains the majority of the world's [most dangerous cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_murder_rate).[^cite_note-298]

Crime and violence prevention and public security are now important issues for governments and citizens in the Latin America region. Homicide rates in Latin America are the highest in the world. From the early 1980s through the mid-1990s, homicide rates increased by 50 percent. Latin America experienced more than 2.5 million murders between 2000 and 2017.[^cite_note-299] There were a total of 63,880 murders in Brazil in 2018.[^cite_note-300]

The most frequent victims of such homicides are young men, 69 percent of them between the ages of 15 and 19. Countries in Latin America and the Caribbean with the highest homicide rate per year per 100,000 inhabitants in 2015 were: El Salvador 109, Honduras 64, Venezuela 57, Jamaica 43, Belize 34.4, St. Kitts and Nevis 34, Guatemala 34, Trinidad and Tobago 31, the Bahamas 30, Brazil 26.7, Colombia 26.5, the Dominican Republic 22, St. Lucia 22, Guyana 19, Mexico 16, Puerto Rico 16, Ecuador 13, Grenada 13, Costa Rica 12, Bolivia 12, Nicaragua 12, Panama 11, Antigua and Barbuda 11, and Haiti 10.[^cite_note-Homicide_Statistics_database.-301] Most of the countries with the highest homicide rates are in Africa and Latin America. Countries in Central America, like El Salvador and Honduras, top the list of homicides in the world.[^cite_note-murder_rates2-302]

Brazil has more overall [homicides](123451.md) than any country in the world, at 50,108, accounting for one in 10 globally. Crime-related violence is the biggest threat to public health in Latin America, striking more victims than [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS) or any other infectious disease.[^cite_note-303] Countries with the lowest homicide rate per year per 100,000 inhabitants as of 2015 were: Chile 3, Peru 7, Argentina 7, Uruguay 8 and Paraguay 9.[^cite_note-Homicide_Statistics_database.-301][^cite_note-304]

## Culture

Main article: [Latin American culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_culture)



Roman Catholic Easter procession in [Comayagua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comayagua), Honduras



Nicaraguan women wearing the [Mestizaje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizaje) costume, which is a traditional costume worn to dance the Mestizaje dance. The costume demonstrates the Spanish influence upon Nicaraguan clothing.

[^cite_note-305]



[Argentine](28820502.md) women making [empanadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empanadas), a traditional [Iberian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) food.

| icon | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Latin_America) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(April 2022)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Latin American culture is a mixture of many influences including cultures from their indigenous people as well as European, African, Asian, and US influences.

Indigenous cultures are that of the people who inhabited the continent prior to European colonization. Ancient and advanced civilizations developed their own political, social and religious systems. The Maya, the Aztec and the Inca are examples of these. Indigenous legacies in music, dance, foods, arts and crafts, clothing, folk culture and traditions are strong in Latin America. Indigenous languages affected Spanish and Portuguese, giving rise to [loanwords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanword) like [pampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pampas), [taco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taco), [tamale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamale), [moccassin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moccassin), [cacique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cacique).

[European cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Europe) were brought mainly by the colonial powers – the Spanish and Portuguese – between the 16th and 19th centuries, including as part of the [Spanish Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Golden_Age). The most enduring European colonial influences are language, institutions, customs and Catholicism.

Additional cultural influences came from Europe during the 18th, 19th and 20th centuries, due to growing immigration from Spain, Portugal, France, Italy and Germany; as well as artistic, ideological and technological developments of the time. Due to the impact of [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) ideals after the French Revolution, a certain number of Iberian American countries decriminalized homosexuality after France and French territories in the Americas did so in 1791.&#x20;

Some of the countries that abolished sodomy laws or banned state interference in consensual adult sexuality in the 19th century were:&#x20;

- Dominican Republic (1822)

- Brazil (1824)

- Peru (1836)

- Mexico (1871)

- Paraguay (1880)

- Argentina (1887)

- Honduras (1899)

- Guatemala

- El Salvador

Today same-sex marriage is legal in Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Ecuador, Mexico, Uruguay, and French overseas departments. South America experienced waves of immigration of Europeans, especially Italians, Spaniards, Portuguese, Germans, Austrians, Poles, Ukrainians, French, Dutch, Russians, Croatians, Lithuanians, and Ashkenazi Jews. With the end of colonialism, French culture also exerted a direct influence in Latin America, especially in the realms of [high culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_culture), [Independentism](62450.md), science and medicine.[^cite_note-306] This can be seen in the region's artistic traditions, including painting, literature, and music, and in the realms of science and politics.

Latin America also sees influences from [African cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Africa), whose presence stems from a long history of the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md). People of African descent have influenced the ethno-scapes of Latin America and the Caribbean. This is manifested for instance in music, dance and religion, especially in countries like Brazil, Puerto Rico, Venezuela, Colombia, Panama, Honduras, Nicaragua, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, and Cuba.

[Asian cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Asia) are also present, which derives from the long history of the [coolies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coolie) who mostly arrived during the 19th and 20th centuries. This was most commonly Chinese workers in Peru and Venezuela, but also from Japanese and Korean immigration- especially headed to Brazil. This has greatly affected cuisine and other traditions including literature, art and lifestyles and politics. Asian influences have especially affected Brazil, Cuba, Panama and Peru.

Lastly, the influence of the United States and globalization is present throughout the region, with particular strength in northern Latin America, especially Puerto Rico, which is an American territory. Prior to 1959, Cuba, which fought for its independence with American aid in the [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War), also had a close political and economic relationship with the United States. The United States also helped Panama become independent from Colombia and built the twenty-mile-long [Panama Canal Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Canal_Zone) in Panama, which it held from 1903—the [Panama Canal](24849.md) opened to transoceanic freight traffic in 1914—to 1999, when the [Torrijos–Carter Treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torrijos%E2%80%93Carter_Treaties) restored Panamanian control of the Canal Zone.

### Art

Main article: [Latin American art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_art)

See also: [List of Latin American artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Latin_American_artists)



[Diego Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Rivera)'s mural depicting Mexico's history at the [National Palace in Mexico City](5124367.md)

Beyond the tradition of Indigenous art, the development of Latin American visual art was influenced by Spanish, Portuguese and French [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque) painting, which in turn often followed the trends of the Italians. In general, artistic [Eurocentrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocentrism) began to wane in the early twentieth century with the increased appreciation for indigenous forms of representation.[^cite_note-307]



Mural by [Santiago Martinez Delgado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Martinez_Delgado) at the [Colombian Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colombian_Congress)

From the early twentieth century, the art of Latin America was greatly inspired by the [Constructivist Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constructivism_\(art\)).[^cite_note-308] The movement rapidly spread from Russia to Europe and then into Latin America. [Joaquín Torres García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_Torres_Garc%C3%ADa) and [Manuel Rendón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Rend%C3%B3n) have been credited with bringing the Constructivist Movement into Latin America from Europe.[^cite_note-309]

An important artistic movement generated in Latin America is *[muralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muralism)* represented by [Diego Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Rivera), [David Alfaro Siqueiros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Alfaro_Siqueiros), [José Clemente Orozco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Clemente_Orozco) and [Rufino Tamayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rufino_Tamayo) in Mexico, [Santiago Martinez Delgado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Martinez_Delgado) and [Pedro Nel Gómez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Nel_G%C3%B3mez) in Colombia and [Antonio Berni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Berni) in Argentina. Some of the most notable *Muralista* works can be found in Mexico, Colombia, New York City, San Francisco, Los Angeles, and [Philadelphia](50585.md).

Painter [Frida Kahlo](162276.md), one of the most famous Mexican artists, painted about her own life and the Mexican culture in a style combining [Realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)), [Symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(arts\)), and [Surrealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealism). Kahlo's work commands the highest selling price of all Latin American paintings.[^cite_note-310]

The Venezuelan [Armando Reverón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armando_Rever%C3%B3n), whose works are recognized internationally, is one of the most important artists of the 20th century in South America; he is a precursor of [Arte Povera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arte_Povera) and [Happening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Happening). In the 60s kinetic art emerged in Venezuela. Its main representatives are [Jesús Soto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jes%C3%BAs_Soto), [Carlos Cruz-Diez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Cruz-Diez), [Alejandro Otero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Otero), and [Gego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gego).

Colombian sculptor and painter [Fernando Botero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Botero) has gained regional and international recognition for his works which, on first examination, are noted for their exaggerated proportions and the corpulence of the human and animal figures.[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313]

The Ecuadorian [Oswaldo Guayasamín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswaldo_Guayasam%C3%ADn) is considered one of the most important and seminal artists in Ecuador and South America. In his life, he made over 13,000 paintings and held more than 180 exhibitions all over the world, including Paris, Barcelona, New York, Buenos Aires, Moscow, Prague, and Rome. He brought his unique style of expressionism and cubism to the collection of Ecuador artwork during the [Age of Anger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Anger) which relates to the period of the Cold War when the United States opposed communist presence in South America.[^cite_note-314] Social criticism of human and social inequality was central to his artwork.[^cite_note-315]

### Film

Main article: [Latin American cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_cinema)



The [Guadalajara International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara_International_Film_Festival) is considered the most prestigious film festival in Latin America.

Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(April 2022)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Latin American film encompasses a wide range of genres and national industries. Historically, the main centers of production have been Mexico, Argentina, Brazil, and Cuba. Latin American film flourished after sound was introduced in cinema, which added a linguistic barrier to the export of Hollywood film south of the border.[^cite_note-316]



In 2015, [Alejandro González Iñárritu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Gonz%C3%A1lez_I%C3%B1%C3%A1rritu) became the second Mexican director in a row to win both the [Academy Award for Best Director](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director) and the [Directors Guild of America Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directors_Guild_of_America_Award) for Best Director. He won his second [Oscar](324.md) in 2016 for *[The Revenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Revenant_\(2015_film\))*.

[Mexican cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Mexico) began in the silent era from 1896 to 1929 and flourished in the [Golden Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_age_of_the_cinema_of_Mexico) of the 1940s. It boasted a huge industry comparable to [Hollywood](418334.md) at the time, with stars such as [María Félix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_F%C3%A9lix), [Dolores del Río](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolores_del_R%C3%ADo), and [Pedro Infante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Infante). In the 1970s, Mexico was the location for many cult horror and action movies. More recently, films such as *[Amores Perros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amores_Perros)* (2000) and *[Y tu mamá también](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y_tu_mam%C3%A1_tambi%C3%A9n)* (2001) enjoyed box office and critical acclaim and propelled [Alfonso Cuarón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_Cuar%C3%B3n) and [Alejandro González Iñárritu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Gonz%C3%A1lez_I%C3%B1%C3%A1rritu) to the front rank of Hollywood directors. Iñárritu in 2010 directed *[Biutiful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biutiful)* and [*Birdman*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birdman_\(film\)) (2014), Alfonso Cuarón directed *[Harry Potter and the Prisoner of Azkaban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Potter_and_the_Prisoner_of_Azkaban_\(film\))* in 2004 and *[Gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity_\(2013_film\))* in 2013. A close friend of both, [Guillermo del Toro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_del_Toro), a top rank Hollywood director in Hollywood and Spain, directed *[Pan's Labyrinth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan's_Labyrinth)* (2006) and produced *[El Orfanato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Orphanage_\(2007_film\))* (2007). [Carlos Carrera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Carrera) (*[The Crime of Father Amaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crime_of_Father_Amaro_\(film\)))*, and screenwriter [Guillermo Arriaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_Arriaga) are also some of the best known modern Mexican film makers. *[Rudo y Cursi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudo_y_Cursi)* released in December (2008) in Mexico, was directed by [Carlos Cuarón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Cuar%C3%B3n).



President [Cristina Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cristina_Fern%C3%A1ndez) with the film director [Juan José Campanella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Campanella) and the cast of *[The Secret in Their Eyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Secret_in_Their_Eyes)* (2009) with the [Oscar for Best Foreign Language Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Foreign_Language_Film)

[Argentine cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Argentina) has also been prominent since the first half of the 20th century and today averages over 60 full-length titles yearly. The industry suffered during the [1976–1983 military dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proceso_de_Reorganizaci%C3%B3n_Nacional); but re-emerged to produce the [Academy Award](324.md) winner *[The Official Story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Official_Story)* in 1985. A wave of imported US films again damaged the industry in the early 1990s, though it soon recovered, thriving even during the [Argentine economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentine_economic_crisis_\(1999-2002\)) around 2001. Many Argentine movies produced during recent years have been internationally acclaimed, including *[Nueve reinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nueve_reinas)* (2000), *[Son of the Bride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_of_the_Bride)* (2001), *[El abrazo partido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_abrazo_partido)* (2004), *[El otro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_otro)* (2007), the 2010 Foreign Language Academy Award winner *[El secreto de sus ojos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_secreto_de_sus_ojos)*, *[Wild Tales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_Tales_\(film\))* (2014) and *[Argentina, 1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentina,_1985)* (2022).

[In Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Brazil), the *[Cinema Novo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_Novo)* movement created a particular way of making movies with critical and intellectual screenplays, clearer photography related to the light of the outdoors in a tropical landscape, and a political message. The modern Brazilian film industry has become more profitable inside the country, and some of its productions have received prizes and recognition in Europe and the United States, with movies such as *[Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil_\(film\))* (1999), *[Cidade de Deus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_God_\(2002_film\))* (2002) and *[Tropa de Elite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropa_de_Elite)* (2007).



Cast of [A Fantastic Woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Fantastic_Woman) on the red carpet at the [Teatro de la Ciudad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_de_la_Ciudad) (City Theatre). It was selected as the Chilean entry for the [Best Foreign Language Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Foreign_Language_Film) where it won in the [90th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/90th_Academy_Awards).

[^cite_note-Premios_Óscar_Latinos-317]

[Puerto Rican cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Puerto_Rico) has produced some notable films, such as *[Una Aventura Llamada Menudo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Una_Aventura_Llamada_Menudo)*, *[Los Diaz de Doris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Diaz_de_Doris)* and *[Casi Casi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casi_Casi)*. An influx of Hollywood films affected the local film industry in Puerto Rico during the 1980s and 1990s, but several Puerto Rican films have been produced since and it has been recovering.

[Cuban cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Cuba) has enjoyed much official support since the [Cuban revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_revolution) and important film-makers include [Tomás Gutiérrez Alea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Guti%C3%A9rrez_Alea).

[Venezuelan television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_television) has also had a great impact in Latin America, is said that whilst "Venezuelan cinema began sporadically in the 1950s\[, it] only emerged as a national-cultural movement in the mid-1970s" when it gained state support and auteurs could produce work. International co-productions with Latin America and Spain continued into this era and beyond, and Venezuelan films of this time were counted among the works of New Latin American Cinema. This period is known as Venezuela's Golden Age of cinema, having massive popularity even though it was a time of much social and political upheaval.

One of the most famous Venezuelan films, even to date, is the 1976 film [Soy un delincuente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soy_un_delincuente) by [Clemente de la Cerda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemente_de_la_Cerda), which won the [Special Jury Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Jury_Prize_\(Locarno_International_Film_Festival\)) at the 1977 [Locarno International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locarno_International_Film_Festival). Soy un delincuente was one of nine films for which the state gave substantial funding to produce, made in the year after the Venezuelan state began giving financial support to cinema in 1975. The support likely came from increased oil wealth in the early 1970s, and the subsequent 1973 credit incentive policy. At the time of its production the film was the most popular film in the country, and took a decade to be usurped from this position, even though it was only one in a string of films designed to tell [social realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_realism) stories of struggle in the 1950s and '60s. Equally famous is the 1977 film [El Pez que Fuma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Pez_que_Fuma) ([Román Chalbaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rom%C3%A1n_Chalbaud)). In 1981 FONCINE (the Venezuelan Film Fund) was founded, and this year it provided even more funding to produce seventeen feature films. A few years later in 1983 with [Viernes Negro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viernes_Negro), oil prices dropped and Venezuela entered a depression which prevented such extravagant funding, but film production continued; more transnational productions occurred, many more with Spain due to Latin America's poor economic fortune in general, and there was some in new cinema, as well: [Fina Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fina_Torres)' 1985 [Oriana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriana_\(film\)) won the [Caméra d'Or](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cam%C3%A9ra_d'Or) Prize at the [1985 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Cannes_Film_Festival) as the best first feature. Film production peaked in 1984–5,<sup>\:37</sup> with 1986 considered Venezuelan cinema's most successful year by the state, thanks to over 4 million admissions to national films, according to [Venezuelanalysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelanalysis). The Venezuelan capital of Caracas hosted the Ibero-American Forum on Cinematography Integration in 1989, from which the pan-continental IBERMEDIA was formed; a union which provides regional funding.

### Literature

Main article: [Latin American literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_literature)

| --- | --- |

See also: [List of Latin American writers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Latin_American_writers)



Chilean poet [Gabriela Mistral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriela_Mistral), first Latin American to win a [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md), in 1945



[Gabriel García Márquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Garc%C3%ADa_M%C3%A1rquez) Nobel Prize in Literature in 1982, primarily for his masterpiece, "One Hundred Years of Solitude" ("Cien años de soledad").



[Octavio Paz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octavio_Paz) Nobel Prize in Literature in 1990 for his influential body of work, which explored existential themes, Mexican identity, and the complexities of modernity



[Miguel Ángel Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_%C3%81ngel_Asturias) received the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1967 for his literary contributions, particularly for his novels that delve into the complexities of Latin American society and its indigenous cultures.



Peruvian [Mario Vargas Llosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Vargas_Llosa), the winner of the 2010 [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md) and the 1994 [Miguel de Cervantes Prize](3930514.md), among others

Pre-Columbian cultures were primarily oral, although the Aztecs and Maya, for instance, produced elaborate [codices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztec_codices). Oral accounts of mythological and religious beliefs were also sometimes recorded after the arrival of European colonizers, as was the case with the [Popol Vuh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popol_Vuh). Moreover, a tradition of oral narrative survives to this day, for instance among the [Quechua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quechua_languages)-speaking population of Peru and the [Quiché (K'iche')](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K'iche'_people) of Guatemala.

From the very moment of Europe's discovery of the continents, early explorers and [conquistadores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquistadores) produced written accounts and crónicas of their experience – such as [Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus)'s letters or [Bernal Díaz del Castillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernal_D%C3%ADaz_del_Castillo)'s description of the conquest of Mexico. During the colonial period, written culture was often in the hands of the church, within which context [Sor Juana Inés de la Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sor_Juana_In%C3%A9s_de_la_Cruz) wrote memorable poetry and philosophical essays. Towards the end of the 18th century and the beginning of the 19th, a distinctive [criollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criollo_\(people\)) literary tradition emerged, including the first novels such as Lizardi's *[El Periquillo Sarniento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Periquillo_Sarniento)* (1816).[^cite_note-318]

The 19th century was a period of "foundational fictions" in critic Doris Sommer's words, novels in the [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) or [Naturalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalism_\(literature\)) traditions that attempted to establish a sense of national identity, and which often focussed on the Indigenous question or the [dichotomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dichotomy) of "civilization or barbarism" (for which see, say, [Domingo Sarmiento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domingo_Faustino_Sarmiento)'s *[Facundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facundo)* (1845), [Juan León Mera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Le%C3%B3n_Mera)'s *Cumandá* (1879), or [Euclides da Cunha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euclides_da_Cunha)'s *[Os Sertões](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Os_Sert%C3%B5es)* (1902)). The 19th century also witnessed the realist work of [Machado de Assis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machado_de_Assis), who made use of surreal devices of metaphor and playful narrative construction, much admired by critic [Harold Bloom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Bloom).



[Jorge Luis Borges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Luis_Borges) in 1951

At the turn of the 20th century, *[modernismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernismo)* emerged, a poetic movement whose founding text was Nicaraguan poet [Rubén Darío](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rub%C3%A9n_Dar%C3%ADo)'s *Azul* (1888). This was the first Latin American literary movement to influence literary culture outside of the region, and was also the first truly Latin American literature, in that national differences were no longer so much at issue. [José Martí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mart%C3%AD), for instance, though a Cuban patriot, also lived in Mexico and the United States and wrote for journals in Argentina and elsewhere.

However, what really put Latin American literature on the global map was no doubt the literary [boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_Boom) of the 1960s and 1970s, distinguished by daring and experimental novels (such as [Julio Cortázar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Cort%C3%A1zar)'s *[Rayuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hopscotch_\(Julio_Cort%C3%A1zar_novel\))* (1963)) that were frequently published in Spain and quickly translated into English. The Boom's defining novel was [Gabriel García Márquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Garc%C3%ADa_M%C3%A1rquez)'s *[Cien años de soledad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Hundred_Years_of_Solitude)* (1967), which led to the association of Latin American literature with [magic realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_realism), though other important writers of the period such as the Peruvian [Mario Vargas Llosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Vargas_Llosa) and [Carlos Fuentes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Fuentes) do not fit so easily within this framework. Arguably, the Boom's culmination was [Augusto Roa Bastos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusto_Roa_Bastos)'s monumental *Yo, el supremo* (1974). In the wake of the Boom, influential precursors such as [Juan Rulfo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Rulfo), [Alejo Carpentier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejo_Carpentier), and above all [Jorge Luis Borges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Luis_Borges) were also rediscovered.

Contemporary literature in the region is varied, ranging from [Paulo Coelho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paulo_Coelho) and [Isabel Allende](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabel_Allende) to [Diamela Eltit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diamela_Eltit), [Giannina Braschi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giannina_Braschi), [Ricardo Piglia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Piglia), or [Roberto Bolaño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Bola%C3%B1o). There has also been considerable attention paid to the genre of [testimonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testimony#Literature), texts produced in collaboration with [subaltern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subaltern_\(postcolonialism\)) subjects such as [Rigoberta Menchú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigoberta_Mench%C3%BA). Finally, a new breed of chroniclers is represented by the more journalistic [Carlos Monsiváis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Monsiv%C3%A1is) and Pedro Lemebel.

The region boasts six [Nobel Prize winners](23385442.md): in addition to the two Chilean poets [Gabriela Mistral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriela_Mistral) (1945) and [Pablo Neruda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Neruda) (1971), there is also the Guatemalan novelist [Miguel Ángel Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_%C3%81ngel_Asturias) (1967), the Colombian writer [Gabriel García Márquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Garc%C3%ADa_M%C3%A1rquez) (1982), the Mexican poet and essayist [Octavio Paz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octavio_Paz) (1990), and the Peruvian novelist [Mario Vargas Llosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Vargas_Llosa) (2010).

### Music and dance

See also: [Music of Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Latin_America), [Latin pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_pop), and [Latin dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_dance)



[Salsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salsa_\(dance\)) dancing in [Cali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cali), Colombia

Latin America has produced many successful worldwide artists in terms of recorded global music sales. Among the most successful have been [Juan Gabriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Gabriel) (Mexico), the only Latin American musician to have sold over 200 million records worldwide,[^cite_note-319] [Gloria Estefan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_Estefan) (Cuba), [Carlos Santana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Santana), [Luis Miguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Miguel) (Mexico), who all have sold over 90 million records, and [Shakira](6479315.md) (Colombia) and [Vicente Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Fern%C3%A1ndez) (Mexico) with over 50 million records sold worldwide. [Enrique Iglesias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Iglesias), although not a Latin American, has also contributed for the success of Latin music.

Other notable successful mainstream acts through the years include [RBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RBD), [Celia Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celia_Cruz), [Soda Stereo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soda_Stereo), [Thalía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thal%C3%ADa), [Ricky Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricky_Martin), [Maná](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man%C3%A1), [Marc Anthony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Anthony), [Ricardo Arjona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Arjona), [Selena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selena), and [Menudo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menudo_\(band\)).

Latin Caribbean music, such as [merengue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merengue_\(music\)), [bachata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachata_\(music\)), [salsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salsa_music), and more recently [reggaeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reggaeton), from countries such as the Dominican Republic, Puerto Rico, Cuba, and Panama, has been strongly influenced by African rhythms and melodies.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321]



Traditional Mexican dance [Jarabe Tapatío](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarabe_Tapat%C3%ADo)

Another well-known Latin American musical genre includes the [Argentine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentine_tango) and [Uruguayan tango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruguayan_tango) (with [Carlos Gardel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Gardel) as the greatest exponent), as well as the distinct [nuevo tango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuevo_tango), a fusion of tango, [acoustic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acoustic_music) and [electronic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_music) popularized by [bandoneón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandone%C3%B3n) virtuoso [Ástor Piazzolla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%81stor_Piazzolla). [Samba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba), North American [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), [European classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_classical_music) and [choro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choro) combined to form *[bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova)* in Brazil, popularized by guitarist [João Gilberto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Gilberto) with singer [Astrud Gilberto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrud_Gilberto) and [pianist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_piano) [Antonio Carlos Jobim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Carlos_Jobim).

Other influential Latin American sounds include the Colombian [cumbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumbia) and [vallenato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vallenato), the Chilean [cueca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cueca), the Ecuadorian [boleros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolero_\(Spanish_dance\)) and [rockoleras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockoleras?action=edit\&redlink=1), the Honduran [punta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punta), the Mexican [ranchera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranchera) and the [mariachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariachi), which is the epitome of Mexican soul, the Nicaraguan [palo de Mayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palo_de_Mayo), the Peruvian [marinera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marinera) and [tondero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondero), the Uruguayan [candombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candombe) and the various styles of music from pre-Columbian traditions that are widespread in the [Andean region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andean_world).



Brazilian singer [Carmen Miranda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmen_Miranda) helped popularize [samba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba) internationally.

The classical composer [Heitor Villa-Lobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heitor_Villa-Lobos) (1887–1959) worked on the recording of Native musical traditions within his homeland of Brazil. The traditions of his homeland heavily influenced his classical works.[^cite_note-322] Also notable is the recent work of the Cuban [Leo Brouwer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Brouwer), Uruguayan-American [Miguel del Águila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_del_%C3%81guila), guitar works of the Venezuelan [Antonio Lauro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Lauro) and the Paraguayan [Agustín Barrios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agust%C3%ADn_Barrios). Latin America has also produced world-class classical performers such as the Chilean pianist [Claudio Arrau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudio_Arrau), Brazilian pianist [Nelson Freire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Freire) and the Argentine pianist and conductor [Daniel Barenboim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Barenboim). Brazilian opera soprano [Bidu Sayão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bidu_Say%C3%A3o), one of Brazil's most famous musicians, was a leading artist of the Metropolitan Opera in New York City from 1937 to 1952.



A couple dances [tango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tango_\(dance\)) in Buenos Aires, Argentina.

A main contribution to music occurred through folklore, where the culture of Latin American and Caribbean countries is expressed. Musicians such as [Yma Súmac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yma_S%C3%BAmac), [Chabuca Granda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chabuca_Granda), [Atahualpa Yupanqui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atahualpa_Yupanqui), [Violeta Parra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violeta_Parra), [Víctor Jara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%ADctor_Jara), [Jorge Cafrune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Cafrune), [Facundo Cabral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facundo_Cabral), [Mercedes Sosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes_Sosa), [Jorge Negrete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Negrete), [Luiz Gonzaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_Gonzaga), [Caetano Veloso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caetano_Veloso), [Susana Baca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susana_Baca), [Chavela Vargas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavela_Vargas), [Simon Diaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Diaz), [Julio Jaramillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_Jaramillo), [Toto la Momposina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toto_la_Momposina), [Gilberto Gil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilberto_Gil), [Maria Bethânia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Beth%C3%A2nia), [Nana Caymmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nana_Caymmi), [Nara Leão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nara_Le%C3%A3o), [Gal Costa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gal_Costa), [Ney Matogrosso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ney_Matogrosso) as well as musical ensembles such as [Inti Illimani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inti_Illimani) and [Los Kjarkas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Kjarkas) are examples of the heights of such contribution.

[Latin pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_pop), including many forms of rock, is popular in Latin America today (see [Spanish language rock and roll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language_rock_and_roll)).[^cite_note-323] A few examples are [Café Tacuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caf%C3%A9_Tacuba), [Soda Stereo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soda_Stereo), [Maná](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man%C3%A1), [Los Fabulosos Cadillacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Fabulosos_Cadillacs), [Rita Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rita_Lee), [Mutantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutantes), [Secos e Molhados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secos_e_Molhados) [Legião Urbana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legi%C3%A3o_Urbana), [Titãs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tit%C3%A3s), [Paralamas do Sucesso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paralamas_do_Sucesso), [Cazuza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cazuza), [Barão Vermelho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar%C3%A3o_Vermelho), [Skank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skank_\(band\)), [Miranda!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miranda!), [Cansei de Ser Sexy or CSS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSS_\(band\)), and Bajo Fondo.

More recently, reggaeton, which blends Jamaican reggae and dancehall with Latin America genres such as [bomba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bomba_\(Puerto_Rico\)) and [plena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plena), as well as [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop), is becoming more popular, in spite of the controversy surrounding its lyrics, dance steps ([Perreo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perreo)) and music videos. It has become very popular among populations with a "migrant culture" influence – both Latino populations in the United States, such as southern Florida and New York City, and parts of Latin America where migration to the United States is common, such as Trinidad and Tobago, Dominican Republic, Colombia, Ecuador, El Salvador, and Mexico.[^cite_note-324]

The word *latinoamericano* emerged in the years following the wars of independence in Spain's former colonies \[...] By the late 1850s, *californios* were writing in newspapers about their membership in *América latina* (Latin America) and *latinoamerica*, calling themselves *Latinos* as the shortened name for their hemispheric membership in *la raza latina* (the Latin race). Reprinting an 1858 opinion piece by a correspondent in Havana on race relations in the Americas, *El Clamor Publico* of Los Angeles surmised that 'two rival races are competing with each other ... the Anglo Saxon and the Latin one \[*la raza latina*].'&#x20;

[doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1016/B978-0-12-822562-2.00150-X](https://doi.org/10.1016%2FB978-0-12-822562-2.00150-X). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-323-98434-8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-323-98434-8). The Neotropical Region is one of the major biogeographic divisions for both plants and animals on Earth. It spans from central Mexico and the Caribbean islands to southern South America. The Neotropical Region contains the largest number of mammal species in the New World, and is the region with the largest number of endemic mammal families.

[^cite_note-225]: &#x20;Nutini, Hugo; Isaac, Barry (2009). *Social Stratification in central Mexico 1500–2000*. University of Texas Press. p. 55. There are basically four operational categories that may be termed ethnic or even racial in Mexico today: (1) güero or blanco (white), denoting European and Near East extraction; (2) criollo (creole), meaning light mestizo in this context but actually of varying complexion; (3) mestizo, an imprecise category that includes many phenotypic variations; and (4) indio, also an imprecise category. These are nominal categories, and neither güero/blanco nor criollo is a widely used term (see Nutini 1997: 230). Nevertheless, there is a popular consensus in Mexico today that these four categories represent major sectors of the nation and that they can be arranged into a rough hierarchy: whites and creoles at the top, a vast population of mestizos in the middle, and Indians (perceived as both a racial and an ethnic component) at the bottom.
